Moduli of truncated shtukas and displays
Eva Viehmann, Torsten Wedhorn

Abstract
. We study moduli spaces of truncated local shtukas and truncated displays and describe them as concrete quotient stacks. To do this, we develop a general formalism of frames that can be applied in both cases and is also used to study prismatic displays and prismatic F F -gauges.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 14D23; Secondary: 14D20, 14D24, 14L15, 14L30, 14G35, 11G18

Introduction

Often cohomology of geometric objects takes the form of a module together with an additional structure. As an example one may think of de Rham cohomology equipped with its Hodge filtration. And sometimes the central objects of study are even themselves “modules with additional structures” such as shtukas. In this paper we start a systematic study of moduli spaces of such “modules with additional structures”. We focus on local shtukas and on displays, even though the general formalism that we develop can also applied to prismatic displays, prismatic Breuil–Kisin modules, or prismatic
F
-gauges as explained in the last section. Here we use two main techniques: Geometrization and truncation.

Geometrization

The idea of geometrization has been used very fruitfully in many areas, in particular recently with
p
-adic cohomology theories. Let us focus here on one aspect: Instead of studying modules over a commutative ring
R
with some additional structure
\Sigma
we look for a geometric object
R^{\Sigma}
such that modules over
R^{\Sigma}
are the same as modules over
R
with additional structure
\Sigma
.

A well-known example are modules
M
over
R
with descending
\mathbb{Z}
-filtrations
\Fil^{\bullet}M
(where we leave the precise definition of descending
\mathbb{Z}
-filtration vague for the moment). The corresponding geometrization is defined as follows. Let the multiplicative group
\mathbb{G}_{m}
act on the affine line
\mathbb{A}^{1}
by
(t,x)\mapsto t^{-1}x
and form the corresponding quotient stack
R^{\Fil}:=[\mathbb{G}_{m,R}\backslash\mathbb{A}^{1}_{R}]
. Then it is well-known (see also Proposition
2.4
below) that a vector bundle over
R^{\Fil}
is the same as a vector bundle over
R
with a descending
\mathbb{Z}
-filtration by subbundles. We have an analogous result in the derived category: One can identify
{\mathcal{D}}_{\textup{qc}}(R^{\Fil})
with the filtered derived category of
R

[
Mou21
]
.

This point of view has in our opinion at least three advantages.

(1)

Algebraic constructions can be studied via geometry: For instance, if a filtered vector bundle over
R
is viewed as a vector bundle over
R^{\Fil}
, its graded vector bundle can be obtained by pullback via the closed immersion
B\mathbb{G}_{m,R}=[\mathbb{G}_{m,R}\backslash\{0\}]\to R^{\Fil}
.

(2)

There is a very natural way to endow these geometrized algebraic structures with additional symmetries by a group scheme
G
: In the above example a filtered module over
R
with
G
-structure can be simply defined as a
G
-bundle over
R^{\Fil}
. Compared to the classical way via Tannakian formalism this works also for group schemes
G
for which one might not be able to identify
G
-bundles over
R
with exact monoidal functors from the category of algebraic representations of
G
on finite projective
R
-modules to the category of vector bundles (see
[
Wed24
, A.28, A.30]
).

(3)

One may obtain via geometrization more natural definitions in those cases where the classical definitions do not yield satisfying results: The category of filtered modules defined as usual is not abelian
[
GM03
, II §5.17]
, but the category of all quasi-coherent modules over
R^{\Fil}
certainly is. So we suggest to redefine a filtered module as a quasi-coherent module over
R^{\Fil}
(see Definition
2.1
). This definition will have several advantages later in the paper.

Let us illustrate the idea of geometrization via another example, which will be central in this paper, namely local shtukas (with one leg). Let
\kappa
be a finite field with
q
elements. Let
G
be a reductive group over
\kappa
. Then a local
G
-shtuka over a
\kappa
-algebra
R
is by definition a
G
-bundle
{\mathscr{E}}
over
R\mathopen{[\![}z\mathclose{]\!]}
together with an isomorphism
\phi^{*}({\mathscr{E}}[1/z])\overset{\sim}{\to}{\mathscr{E}}[1/z]
, where
\phi
is the partial Frobenius

(0.0.1)

\phi(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}z^{n})=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}^{q}z^{n}

on
R\mathopen{[\![}z\mathclose{]\!]}
. This structure is now geometrized as follows. Let
R^{\Hck}
be the scheme obtained by gluing two copies of
\Spec R\mathopen{[\![}z\mathclose{]\!]}
along the open subscheme
\Spec R\mathopen{(\!(}z\mathclose{)\!)}
. Then a
G
-bundle on
R^{\Hck}
is the same as a Hecke triple
({\mathscr{E}}_{1},{\mathscr{E}}_{2},\alpha)
, where
{\mathscr{E}}_{1}
and
{\mathscr{E}}_{2}
are
G
-bundles and
\alpha\colon{\mathscr{E}}_{1}[1/z]\overset{\sim}{\to}{\mathscr{E}}_{2}[1/z]
is an isomorphism.

To obtain a geometrization of
G
-shtukas, we denote by
\tau\colon\Spec R\mathopen{[\![}z\mathclose{]\!]}\to R^{\Hck}
the inclusion onto one of the copies of
\Spec R\mathopen{[\![}z\mathclose{]\!]}
and by
\sigma
the inclusion onto the other copy precomposed with
\phi
. Given a
G
-bundle on
R^{\Hck}
viewed as a triple
({\mathscr{E}}_{1},{\mathscr{E}}_{2},\alpha)
, its pullback via
\tau
(resp. via
\sigma
) is
{\mathscr{E}}_{1}
(resp.
\phi^{*}{\mathscr{E}}_{2}
). Hence we define

R^{\Sht}:=\mathop{\textup{colim}}(\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 23.63086pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-23.63086pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Spec R\mathopen{[\![}z\mathclose{]\!]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 28.38678pt\raise 6.65971pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\tau}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 47.63086pt\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 27.91374pt\raise-6.65971pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\sigma}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 47.63086pt\raise-2.15277pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 47.63086pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{R^{\Hck}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces),

where we take the coequalizer in some
\infty
-category containing the classifying stacks
BG
of reductive groups
G
. Then
G
-bundles on
R^{\Sht}
can be identified with local
G
-shtukas over
R
.

Truncation

The second main technique is to approximate the objects that we are interested in (in this paper, mainly local shtukas and displays) by their truncations. For local shtukas and the corresponding Hecke triples this takes the following shape: The naive mod-
z^{N}
-reduction of a Hecke triple or a local shtuka loses to much information, we would essentially end up with a (pair of)
G
-bundles over
R\mathopen{[\![}z\mathclose{]\!]}/(z^{N})
. Instead, we pass to a “completion” (in the sense of compactification) of the stacks
R^{\Hck}
and
R^{\Sht}
. Let us explain this for
R^{\Hck}
, since then one obtains the completion of
R^{\Sht}
again by taking co-equalizers.

If
R
is a field, then
R^{\Hck}
is obtained from gluing two copies of a complete discrete valuation ring along their generic point. It is a regular one-dimensional scheme over
R\mathopen{[\![}z\mathclose{]\!]}
. Now we aim to enrich its special fiber without changing the groupoid of
G
-bundles over it. For this we add a point of codimension
2
. Of course this makes only sense in the world of algebraic stacks in which negative dimensions exist. More precisely, we define for arbitrary
R
the quotient stack

R^{\Hck,\infty}=[\mathbb{G}_{m}\backslash\Spec R\mathopen{[\![}z\mathclose{]\!]}[t,u]/(tu-z)],

where
\mathbb{G}_{m}
acts on
t
(resp.
u
) with degree
-1
(resp.
1
). If
R
is a field, such algebraic stacks have also been studied in detail in
[
AHLH23
]
.

There is an open embedding
R^{\Hck}\to R^{\Hck,\infty}
identifying
R^{\Hck}
with the locus where
u
or
t
is invertible. Pullback via this open immersion defines a functor from the groupoid
\Bun_{G}(R^{\Hck,\infty})
of
G
-bundles on
R^{\Hck,\infty}
to
\Bun_{G}(R^{\Hck})
. General results on extension of
G
-bundles (e.g.,
[
Wed24
]
) show that this functor is fully faithful and even an equivalence if
R
is a field (Proposition
6.3
). For general
R
one can also describe the essential image of the functor (Proposition
5.14
for vector bundles, Corollary
5.16
for general
G
-bundles).

Now we can also define truncated Hecke stacks
R^{\Hck,N}
for
N\geq 1
by replacing
R\mathopen{[\![}z\mathclose{]\!]}
with
R\mathopen{[\![}z\mathclose{]\!]}/(z^{N})
in (*). Via a very similar coequalizer construction we also obtain truncated shtuka stacks
R^{\Sht,N}
(Definition
6.18
). By definition, an
N
-truncated local
G
-shtuka over
R
is then a
G
-bundle on
R^{\Sht,N}
. In Theorem
6.21
we see

\Bun_{G}(R^{\Sht,\infty})=\lim_{N<\infty}\Bun_{G}(R^{\Sht,N}).

Hence we may approximate local
G
-shtukas by truncated local
G
-shtukas. In fact, we deduce this result from the fact that one has

\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{N<\infty}R^{\Sht,N}=R^{\Sht,\infty},

where we form the colimit in the 2-category of Adams stacks, whose theory we recall in Appendix
A
.

We also show that the moduli space of truncated local
G
-shtukas decomposes as a direct sum of open and closed connected components parametrized by conjugacy classes
[\mu]
of cocharacters of
G
. For each connected component we give a concrete description as a quotient stack (Theorem
6.23
). This is an application of the general classification result explained below (Theorem
4
).

Finally we relate 1-truncated local
G
-shtukas to
G
-zips as defined in
[
PWZ15
]
again by geometrization: Let
R^{\Zip}
be the algebraic stack defined by Yaylali
[
Yay24
]
such that
G
-bundles over
R^{\Zip}
can be identified with
G
-zips (Definition
6.25
). Then there are universal homeomorphisms
R^{\Sht,1}\to R^{\Zip}\to R^{\Sht,1}
(Proposition
6.26
).

General results

Most of the above results on (truncated) local shtukas exist also for (truncated) displays. In addition, it is also possible to study prismatic displays, prismatic Breuil–Kisin modules, or prismatic
F
-gauges with these techniques with analogous arguments. Therefore we develop a general formalism such that all of the above cases are special cases of our formalism.

Quotient stacks by
\mathbb{G}_{m}

Since all of our geometrizations are algebraic quotient stacks by
\mathbb{G}_{m}
we start by studying these stacks, their fixed point locus, attractor locus, and repeller locus (Section
1.2
). Then we recall some facts about quasi-coherent modules, modules of finite presentation and vector bundles on such stacks (Section
1.3
).

Finally, we consider colimits of such stacks. To keep it a purely algebraic theory and to avoid using GAGA theorems comparing
G
-bundles over formal schemes and over algebraic schemes we here work in the 2-category of Adams stacks developed by Schäppi and recalled in Appendix
A
. The results of Schäppi also give us a tool at hand to identify colimits of such stacks (Theorem
A.7
). We use this to describe certain colimits of quotient stacks by
\mathbb{G}_{m}
(Proposition
1.9
).

Filtered rings and their Rees stacks

We then develop a general formalism of filtered modules and filtered algebras. According to our principle of geometrization, a filtered module over a ring
A
is defined as a quasi-coherent module over the quotient stack
A^{\Fil}
(Definition
2.1
). We describe the attached derived graded module. We describe vector bundles over
A^{\Fil}
and show that they are simply vector bundles with a filtration by subbundles (Proposition
2.4
).

Next we study filtered rings, where we only consider filtrations in non-negative degrees. Thus the natural definition according to our principles is the following.

Definition 1
.

A (non-negatively graded) filtered ring with underlying ring
A
is a quasi-coherent algebra over
A^{\Fil}
whose underlying
A
-algebra is
A
and such that its derived graded module is concentrated in non-negative degrees.

This can also be made more explicit (Section
2.2
) as a sequence of (not necessarily injective maps) maps of
A
-modules

\cdots\mathrel{\smash{\overset{t}{\longrightarrow}}}\Fil^{2}\mathrel{\smash{\overset{t}{\longrightarrow}}}\Fil^{1}\mathrel{\smash{\overset{t}{\longrightarrow}}}\Fil^{0}=A

endowed with a multiplication and satisfying certain compatibilities.

Every quasi-coherent algebra
{\mathscr{B}}
over
A^{\Fil}
, given by a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
A
-algebra
B
, corresponds to an affine morphism
\Re(B)=\Spec{\mathscr{B}}\to A^{\Fil}
, and
\Re(B)
is called the
Rees stack
. We describe the fixed point, the attractor, and the repeller locus of the Rees stacks (Remark
2.13
). For a filtered ring
(A,(\Fil^{j}))
, the complement of the attractor locus can be identified with
\Spec A
and one obtains an open immersion

\tau\colon\Spec A\longrightarrow\Re(A,(\Fil^{j})).

Frames

We develop a general formalism of frames that generalizes the notion of higher frames defined by Lau
[
Lau21
]
.

Definition 2
.

(Definition
4.1
)
A
frame
is a filtered ring
(A,(\Fil^{j})_{j})
together with a map of algebraic stacks

\sigma\colon\Spec A\longrightarrow\Re(A,(\Fil^{j}))

which factors through the complement of the repeller locus.

The composition of
\sigma
with the structure morphism
\Re(A,(\Fil^{j}))\to\Spec A
corresponds then to a ring endomorphism of
A
which is denoted by
\phi
.

As in the special case of local shtukas we thus obtain the attached
frame stack

{\mathcal{F}}(A):=\mathop{\textup{colim}}(\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 17.1117pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-17.1117pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Spec A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 21.86761pt\raise 6.65971pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\tau}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 41.1117pt\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 21.39458pt\raise-6.65971pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\sigma}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 41.1117pt\raise-2.15277pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 41.1117pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Re(A,(\Fil^{j}))}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces)

These constructions indeed generalize the local shtuka case explained above:

Example 3
.

Let
\kappa
be a finite field with
q
elements, let
R
be a
\kappa
-algebra, and for
1\leq N\leq\infty
set
R_{N}:=R\mathopen{[\![}z\mathclose{]\!]}/(z^{N})
. Consider the filtered ring

\cdot\mathrel{\smash{\overset{\cdot z}{\longrightarrow}}}R_{N}\mathrel{\smash{\overset{\cdot z}{\longrightarrow}}}R_{N}\mathrel{\smash{\overset{\cdot z}{\longrightarrow}}}R_{N}

defining the Rees stack
\Re(R_{N},z)
. Then the complement
\Re(R_{N},z)^{\neq-}
of the repeller locus is canonically isomorphic to
\Spec R_{N}
and we define
\sigma
as the composition

\sigma\colon\Spec R_{N}\mathrel{\smash{\overset{\phi}{\longrightarrow}}}\Spec R_{N}\cong\Re(R_{N},z)^{\neq-}\longrightarrow\Re(R_{N},z),

where
\phi
is given by (
0.0.1
). Then
(R_{N},z,\sigma)
is a frame and we obtain the associated frame stack
{\mathcal{F}}(R_{N},z)
. There are functorial isomorphisms

\Re(R_{N},z)\cong R^{\Hck,N},\qquad{\mathcal{F}}(R_{N},z)\cong R^{\Sht,N}.

Moreover, for
N=\infty
, the complement of the fixed locus in
R^{\Hck,\infty}
is
R^{\Hck}
, and the corresponding substack of
R^{\Sht,\infty}
is
R^{\Sht}
.

This example is a special case of frames attached to filtered rings given by
A
-linear maps
v\colon L\to A
, where
L
is an invertible module over a ring
A
. This is also of interest in the prismatic context. We consider it in detail in Section
5
.

Moduli spaces of
G
-bundles on frame stacks

Our main object of study are moduli spaces of
G
-bundles over frame stacks for reductive groups
G
(in fact, many of our results hold for more general group schemes). For this we study frames in families. More precisely, we consider a functor
A
that sends an object
R
in a base category (below this will be the category of algebras over some fixed ring) to a pair consisting of a frame
(A_{R},(\Fil_{A_{R}}^{j}),\sigma_{R})
and an isomorphism
A_{R}/t(\Fil^{1}_{A_{R}})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}R
. We obtain for any stack
{\mathcal{X}}
the attached “loop prestack”
L^{A}{\mathcal{X}}
defined by
R\mapsto{\mathcal{X}}(A_{R})
. In the local shtuka case we have
A_{R}=R\mathopen{[\![}z\mathclose{]\!]}
and in this case,
L^{A}{\mathcal{X}}
is the positive loop stack of
{\mathcal{X}}
. We assume that the following conditions are satisfied.

(a)

R\mapsto\Fil^{j}_{A_{R}}
is representable by an affine scheme for all
j
.

(b)

(A_{R},t(\Fil^{1}_{A_{R}}))
is a henselian pair.

(c)

BL^{A}G\to L^{A}BG
is an isomorphism.

They are satisfied in the explicit contexts discussed below and at the same time allow us to first abstractly discuss general classification results.
For every reductive group
G
we obtain the prestacks of
G
-bundles

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{B}\textup{un}}_{G}(A)\colon R

\displaystyle\mapsto\Bun_{G}\left(\Re(A_{R},(\Fil^{j}_{A_{R}}))\right),

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{B}\textup{un}}_{G}({\mathcal{F}}(A))\colon R

\displaystyle\mapsto\Bun_{G}\left({\mathcal{F}}(A_{R},(\Fil^{j}_{A_{R}})),\sigma_{R}\right).

The above hypotheses then imply that these are stacks for the fpqc topology. We show that both of these stacks admit decompositions into open and closed substacks

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{B}\textup{un}}_{G}(A)

\displaystyle=\coprod_{[\mu]}\underline{\mathcal{B}\textup{un}}^{[\mu]}_{G}(A),

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{B}\textup{un}}_{G}({\mathcal{F}}(A))

\displaystyle=\coprod_{[\mu]}\underline{\mathcal{B}\textup{un}}^{[\mu]}_{G}({\mathcal{F}}(A)),

where
[\mu]
runs through the conjugacy classes of cocharacters of
G
. In this introduction only we ignore the notational problem that there might exist conjugacy classes that are not defined over the same ring over which
G
is defined. We attach to the functor
R\mapsto(A_{R},(\Fil_{A_{R}}^{j}))
and to a pair
(G,[\mu])
a functor of groups
E_{A}(G,[\mu])
(
3.3.1
) which is called the display group and which can be shown to be an affine group scheme (not necessarily of finite type) using Assumption
(a)
. Then the next result is our main general classification result.

Theorem 4
.

(Theorem
3.29
, Theorem
4.12
)
One has equivalences of stacks

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{B}\textup{un}}^{[\mu]}_{G}(A)

\displaystyle\cong BE_{A}(G,[\mu]),

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{B}\textup{un}}^{[\mu]}_{G}({\mathcal{F}}(A))

\displaystyle\cong[E_{A}(G,\mu)\backslash L^{A}G],

where the action of the affine group scheme
E_{A}(G,\mu)
on
L^{A}G
is induced by
\sigma
and
\tau
(
4.3.4
).

The proof of the first equivalence uses the Assumption
(b)
and results in
[
Wed24
]
, recalled in the form used here in Corollary
3.3
. The second equivalence follows then quite formally using Assumption
(c)
.

Rees stacks and frames given by the
v
-adic filtration

The underlying filtered rings for (truncated) shtukas, for (truncated) displays over perfect rings, and for prismatic displays and prismatic
F
-gauges are all given by the so-called
v
-adic filtration, where
A
is a ring and
v\colon L\to A
is an
A
-linear map from a line bundle
L
over
A
. Any ring endomorphism
\phi
of
A
induces a frame structure. The attached frame stack is denoted by
{\mathcal{F}}(A,v,\phi)
. In Section
5
we discuss this example in detail. We describe vector bundles on the attached Rees stack
\Re(A,v)
(Proposition
5.8
). Moreover, we obtain the following result.

Theorem 5
.

(Corollary
5.19
)
Let
v
be injective and let
{\mathcal{F}}(A,v,\phi)^{\neq 0}
be the open complement of the fixed point locus. Then for every smooth affine group scheme
G
over
A
, pullback via the inclusion yields a fully faithful functor

(0.0.2)

\Bun_{G}({\mathcal{F}}(A,v,\phi))\longrightarrow\Bun_{G}({\mathcal{F}}(A,v,\phi)^{\neq 0}).

We can describe its essential image. Moreover (
0.0.2
) is an equivalence if
G
is reductive,
A
is a discrete valuation ring, and
v
is the inclusion of the maximal ideal of
A
.

In the local shtuka case, (
0.0.2
) describes the restriction of
\infty
-truncated local
G
-shtukas to local
G
-shtukas. In the prismatic case, (
0.0.2
) describes the functor from prismatic
G
-displays to Breuil–Kisin
G
-modules (Section
8
).

Further applications

Beyond the case of local shtukas we also apply our general formalism to displays and to prisms.

Moduli spaces of truncated
G
-displays

In the case of displays we rephrase, extend, and geometrize results by Lau and Daniels. The general formalism developed in the first four sections allows us to define for every
p
-adically complete ring
R
stacks
R^{\Disp}
and truncations
R^{\Disp,N,n}
for integers
N>n\geq 1
. If
R
is a ring of characteristic
p
, then we can also define truncations for
N=n
, and the stack is then denoted by
R^{\Disp,N}
. A (
(N,n)
-truncated)
G
-display over
R
is then by definition a
G
-bundle over
R^{\Disp}
(over
R^{\Disp,N,n}
). In an appendix, Christopher Lang proves that
\GL_{h}
-displays of type
[\mu_{d}]
, where
\mu_{d}
is the cocharacter

(0.0.3)

t\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}tI_{d}&0\\
0&I_{h-d}\end{pmatrix}

are indeed the same as
(\GL_{h},\mu_{d})
-displays as defined by Bültel and Pappas, justifying our definition.

Then Theorem
4
allows us to describe the moduli space of
G
-displays (resp. of
(N,n)
-truncated
G
-displays) as a quotient stack of the positive Witt loop group by the action of a display group (Theorem
7.9
) recovering results of Lau in
[
Lau21
]
. This description also implies that the moduli space of
G
-displays is the limit of the moduli space of truncated
G
-displays (Corollary
7.10
).

In this case, the following proposition implies that
1
-truncated
G
-displays are in fact the same as
G
-zips.

Proposition 6
.

Let
R
be a ring of characteristic
p
. Then there is a canonical isomorphism of algebraic stacks
R^{\Zip}\overset{\sim}{\to}R^{\Disp,1}
.

Existence of Traverso bounds

Both in the shtuka case and in the display case we can now approximate local
G
-shtukas (resp.
G
-displays) by their truncations. For displays without additional structure choose
G=\GL_{h}
and
\mu=\mu_{d}
as in (
0.0.3
). Then Dieudonné theory yields an identification between (
N
-truncated)
p
-divisible groups of height
h
and dimension
d
over an algebraically closed field
k
of characteristic
p>0
and (
N
-truncated)
\GL_{h}
-displays of type
\mu_{d}
over
k
. In this case, it is a classical result that the isomorphism class of such a
p
-divisible group depends only on its
N_{0}
-truncation for some
N_{0}
depending only on
h
and
d
. These bounds have been studied by Traverso
[
Tra69
]
and by Lau, Nicole, and Vasiu
[
LNV13
]
. Here we vastly generalize this result using our descriptions of the moduli spaces to arbitary reductive groups
G
, arbitrary conjugacy classes
[\mu]
, and both for local shtukas and for displays:

Theorem 7
.

(Theorem
6.31
, Theorem
7.14
)
Let
G
be a reductive group and let
[\mu]
be a conjugacy class of cocharacters of
G
. Let
k
be an algebraically closed field of characteristic
p>0
. Then there exists an
N_{0}\geq 1
, dependent only on
G
and
[\mu]
, such that any two
G
-shtukas of type
[\mu]
(or any two
G
-displays of type
[\mu]
) over
k
are isomorphic if and only if their
N_{0}
-truncations are isomorphic.

Prismatic displays and prismatic
F
-gauges

In the last section we apply the general results to filtered rings and frames obtained from a bounded prism
\underline{A}=(A,\delta,I)
. The first case is to consider the
I
-adic filtration. This is a special case of Section
5
. The corresponding frame stack is denoted by
\underline{A}^{\Disp}
. If
\underline{A}
is the prism attached to a perfect ring
R
in characteristic
p
, we have
\underline{A}^{\Disp}=R^{\Disp}
, justifying the notation. An immediate application of Theorem
5
yields a fully faithful functor from the groupoid of
G
-displays over
\underline{A}
and Breuil-Kisin
G
-bundles over
\underline{A}
and determines its essential image (Proposition
8.4
). This reproves (and slightly generalizes) a result of Ito
[
Ito24
, 5.3.8]
.

The second filtration that we consider is the Nygaard filtration and the attached Nygaard frame. The corresponding frame stack is called the syntomic frame stack of
\underline{A}
and is denoted by
\underline{A}^{\Syn}
. This notation is justified by the fact, proven by Bhatt, that if
\underline{A}
is the initial object in the prismatic site of a quasi-regular semi-perfectoid ring
R
, then
\underline{A}^{\Syn}\cong R^{\Syn}
, where
R^{\Syn}
denotes (an algebraic version of) the syntomification of
R
defined by Bhatt and Lurie
[
BL22
]
. Then a prismatic
G
-gauge over
\underline{A}
is by definition a
G
-bundle on
\underline{A}^{\Syn}
. We construct a map of stacks
\underline{A}^{\Disp}\to\underline{A}^{\Syn}
that yields by pullback a functor attaching prismatic
G
-displays to prismatic
G
-gauges which may be seen as the extension of a result of Ito (Remark
8.10
).

Acknowledgements.

We thank Christopher Lang, Timo Richarz, and Can Yaylali for helpful remarks. This project was supported by the Deutsche Forschungsgemeinschaft (DFG, German Research Foundation) via TRR 326 and the Cluster of Excellence Mathematics Münster.

Notation

We always denote by
p
a prime.

Although we will not need the full machinery of
\infty
-categories, it is convenient to consider ordinary categories or
(2,1)
-categories as special cases of
\infty
-categories via their nerves. Similarly, we consider ordinary groupoids as special cases of
\infty
-groupoids a.k.a. anima. For instance if we have a site
{\mathcal{C}}
(in the classical sense), a functor on
{\mathcal{C}}
with values in groupoids or in
(2,1)
-categories is a sheaf if it is a sheaf when considered with values in the
\infty
-category of anima or of
\infty
-categories. This puts us firmly in the frame work of
[
Lur09
]
. An equivalent approach without using the language of
\infty
-categories can be found in
[
Sta25
]
.

Let
S
be a scheme. As usual we denote by
\mathcal{A}\textup{ff/$S$}
the full subcategory of the category of
S
-schemes consisting of scheme morphisms
X\to S
with
X
an affine scheme. A (classical) prestack over
S
is a functor of
(2,1)
-categories from the category
\mathcal{A}\textup{ff/$S$}
to the
(2,1)
-category of groupoids.

A stack over
S
is a prestack over
S
that satisfies descent for the fppf-topology. We call a stack
{\mathcal{X}}

algebraic
if its diagonal is representable by algebraic spaces and if there exists a surjective and smooth morphism
U\to{\mathcal{X}}
, where
U
is a scheme.
We denote by
|{\mathcal{X}}|
the underlying topological space of
{\mathcal{X}}
.

Let
{\mathcal{X}}
and
{\mathcal{Y}}
be stacks over a stack
{\mathcal{S}}
. We denote by
\Hom_{{\mathcal{S}}}({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{Y}})
the groupoid of morphisms
{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{Y}}
of stacks over
S
. The stack
T\mapsto\Hom_{S}({\mathcal{X}}\times_{S}T,{\mathcal{Y}})
is denoted by
\underline{\Hom}_{S}({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{Y}})
. More generally, let
{\mathcal{X}}\colon T\mapsto{\mathcal{X}}_{T}
be a functor from the category
\mathcal{A}\textup{ff/$S$}
to the
(2,1)
-category of stacks over
S
. Then we denote by
\underline{\Hom}_{S}({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{Y}})
the prestack
T\mapsto\Hom_{S}({\mathcal{X}}_{T},{\mathcal{Y}})
. This is a stack, if
T\mapsto{\mathcal{X}}_{T}
is a sheaf for the fppf topology.

If
{\mathcal{X}}
is a pre-stack, then we have the notion of an
{\mathscr{O}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
-module, see
[
Sta25
, 06WB]
. Via the tensor product it is a symmetric monoidal category. A vector bundle on
{\mathcal{X}}
is a finite locally free
{\mathscr{O}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
-module in the sense of
[
Sta25
, 03DE]
. We denote the exact monoidal category of vector bundles on
{\mathcal{X}}
by
\textup{Vec}{{\mathcal{X}}}
.

Let
S
be an algebraic space and let
G
be a group algebraic space over
S
such that
G\to S
is an fpqc covering (see
[
Wed24
, App. A]
), e.g., if
G\to S
is quasi-compact and flat or if
G\to S
is flat and locally of finite presentation. Then for every stack
{\mathcal{X}}
over
S
we denote by
\Bun_{G}({\mathcal{X}})
the groupoid of morphism
{\mathcal{X}}\to BG
over
S
, where
BG
is the classifying stack of
G
. The pointed set of isomorphism classes of objects in
\Bun_{G}({\mathcal{X}})
is denoted by
H^{1}({\mathcal{X}},G)
. We refer to
[
Wed24
, Appendix A]
for more details on
G
-bundles on stacks.

Let
\phi\colon A\to B
be a map of rings. For any
A
-module
M
we set
\phi^{*}(M)=B\otimes_{A}M
. If
N
is a
B
-module and
u\colon M\to N
is a
\phi
-linear map (i.e., an additive map satisfying
u(am)=\phi(a)u(m)
for
a\in A
and
m\in M
), then we denote the corresponding
B
-linear map
\phi^{*}(M)\to N
,
b\otimes m\mapsto bu(m)
by
u^{\#}
.

1.
Quotient stacks by
\mathbb{G}_{m}

1.1.
Describing quotients by
\mathbb{G}_{m}
in the affine case

Let
A
be a ring. Recall that the functor
B\mapsto\Spec B
induces a contravariant equivalence of the category of
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
A
-algebras and the category of affine
A
-schemes with a
\mathbb{G}_{m,A}
-action
[
SGA70
, Exp. I, 4.7.3.1]
. The
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-equivariant morphism
\Spec B\to\Spec A
, where
\Spec A
is endowed with the trivial
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action, induces an affine morphism of algebraic stacks

{\mathcal{X}}:=[\mathbb{G}_{m,A}\backslash(\Spec B)]\to B\mathbb{G}_{m,A}

sitting in a Cartesian diagram

\textstyle{\Spec B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Spec A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\mathbb{G}_{m,A}.}

Conversely, every affine morphism
{\mathcal{X}}\to B\mathbb{G}_{m,A}
yields by base change an affine scheme
\Spec B
over
\Spec A
with
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action, i.e., a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
A
-algebra.

Let us describe the points of
{\mathcal{X}}
:

Remark 1.1
.

If
C
is any
A
-algebra, the groupoid of maps
f\colon\Spec C\to{\mathcal{X}}
over
\Spec A
is the groupoid of pairs
(L,\alpha)
, where

(a)

L
is a projective
C
-module of rank
1
(a line bundle on
C
) corresponding to the
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-bundle
\Spec T(L)
with
T(L):=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}L^{\otimes n}
over
C
and

(b)

\alpha\colon B\to T(L)
is a map of graded
A
-algebras or, equivalently, a map of graded
C
-algebras
C\otimes_{A}B\to T(L)
.

Then
\Spec T(L)
is the underlying
C
-scheme of the
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-bundle corresponding to the line bundle
L
and one has a cartesian diagram

(1.1.1)

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern Example 1.2
.

Let
A
be a ring, let
d\in\mathbb{Z}
be an integer, and let
A[t]
be the polynomial ring which we endow with a
\mathbb{Z}
-grading by setting
\deg(t):=d
. We denote the corresponding
\mathbb{G}_{m,A}
-scheme by
(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{A})^{(d)}
. Let
C
be an
A
-algebra. Then the groupoid
[\mathbb{G}_{m,A}\backslash(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{A})^{(d)}](C)
is the groupoid of pairs
(M,v)
, where
M
is a line bundle on
C
and
v\colon C\to M^{\otimes d}
is a
C
-linear map (equivalently, an element in
M^{\otimes d}
). For
d=-1
, we usually consider
v
has a
C
-linear map
M\to C
.

More generally, let
L
be a line bundle on
A
and consider
\Sym_{A}L=\bigoplus_{j\geq 0}L^{\otimes i}
which we endow with a
\mathbb{Z}
-grading by letting
L
sit in degree
d
to get a
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-scheme
(\Sym_{A}L)^{(d)}
. Then for every
A
-algebra
C
the groupoid
[(\Sym_{A}L)^{(d)}/\mathbb{G}_{m,A}](C)
is the groupoid of pairs
(M,v)
, where
M
is a line bundle on
C
and
v\colon L\otimes_{A}C\to M^{\otimes d}
is a
C
-linear map.

Remark 1.3
.

Let
A
be a ring, let
B
and
C
be
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
A
-algebras, and set
{\mathcal{X}}:=[\mathbb{G}_{m,A}\backslash(\Spec B)]
and
{\mathcal{Y}}:=[\mathbb{G}_{m,A}\backslash\Spec C]
. One has equivalences of groupoids

\displaystyle\Hom_{B\mathbb{G}_{m,A}}({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{Y}})

\displaystyle\cong\Hom^{\mathbb{G}_{m,A}}_{A}(\Spec B,\Spec C)

\displaystyle\cong\{\text{$\mathbb{Z}$-graded maps $C\to B$ of $A$-algebras}\},

where
\Hom^{\mathbb{G}_{m,A}}_{A}(-,-)
denotes the set of
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-equivariant morphisms of
A
-schemes. This shows in particular that the groupoid
\Hom_{B\mathbb{G}_{m,A}}({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{Y}})
is a set.

1.2.
The attractor and the repeller locus

We will consider the following schemes over
A
with
\mathbb{G}_{m,A}
-action.

(1)

\mathbb{P}^{1}_{A}
with its canonical
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action.

(2)

\mathbb{A}^{1}_{A}=(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{A})^{+}
as the open
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-invariant subscheme
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{A}\setminus\{\infty\}
.

(3)

(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{A})^{-}
as the open
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-invariant subscheme
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{A}\setminus\{0\}
.

(4)

\mathbb{G}_{m,A}
as the open
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-invariant subscheme
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{A}\setminus\{0,\infty\}
.

(5)

S=\Spec A
with the trivial
\mathbb{G}_{m,A}
-action.

Let
X
be a scheme with
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action over
A
. Define sheaves for the fpqc topology with
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action on the category of
A
-algebras by

\displaystyle X^{0}

\displaystyle:=\underline{\Hom}_{A}^{\mathbb{G}_{m}}(\Spec A,X),

\displaystyle X^{+}

\displaystyle:=\underline{\Hom}_{A}^{\mathbb{G}_{m}}((\mathbb{A}^{1}_{A})^{+},X),

\displaystyle X^{-}

\displaystyle:=\underline{\Hom}_{A}^{\mathbb{G}_{m}}((\mathbb{A}^{1}_{A})^{-},X),

called the
space of fixed points
,
the attractor
, and
the repeller
, respectively. Note that
\underline{\Hom}_{A}^{\mathbb{G}_{m}}(\mathbb{G}_{m},X)=X
by restricting a
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-equivariant map
f\colon\mathbb{G}_{m}\to X
to its evaluation
f(1)
in the unit section of
\mathbb{G}_{m}
. This description shows that the automorphism
(-)^{-1}
of
\mathbb{G}_{m}
induces the identity on
\underline{\Hom}_{A}^{\mathbb{G}_{m}}(\mathbb{G}_{m},X)=X
.

The zero section of
(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{A})^{+}
and the
\infty
-section of
(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{A})^{-}
are
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-equivariant and hence yield by functoriality
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-equivariant maps

X^{+}\longrightarrow X^{0},\qquad X^{-}\longrightarrow X^{0}.

Similarly, the inclusions
\mathbb{G}_{m,A}\to(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{A})^{+}
and
\mathbb{G}_{m,A}\to(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{A})^{-}
induce
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-equivariant maps

X^{+}\longrightarrow X,\qquad X^{-}\longrightarrow X.

If
X
is separated, then
X^{+}\to X
and
X^{-}\to X
is a monomorphism since
\mathbb{G}_{m}
is schematically dense in
(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{A})^{+}
and in
(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{A})^{-}
. The
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action on
X
is called
attracting
(resp.
repelling
) if
X^{+}\to X
(resp.
X^{-}\to X
) is an isomorphism.

We will be mostly interested in the affine case. Then attractor, reppeler, and fixed point locus have the following explicit description.

Example 1.4
.

Let
A
be a ring and let
B=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}B_{n}
be a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
A
-algebra. Let
I^{+},I^{-}\subseteq B
be the graded ideals generated by
B_{>0}
and
B_{<0}
respectively and let
I^{0}=I^{+}+I^{-}
be the ideal generated by
B_{>0}+B_{<0}
. The ideals
I^{\pm}=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}I^{\pm}_{n}
are explicitly given by

(1.2.1)

\begin{gathered}I^{\pm}_{n}=\begin{cases}B_{n},&\text{if $\pm n>0$};\\
\sum_{i>0}B_{\pm i}B_{\mp i+n},&\text{if $\pm n\leq 0$.}\end{cases}\end{gathered}

One obtains that

(1.2.2)

\begin{gathered}I^{0}_{n}=\begin{cases}B_{n},&\text{if $n\neq 0$};\\
\sum_{i>0}B_{-i}B_{i},&\text{if $n=0$}\end{cases}\end{gathered}

Set
B^{+}:=B/I^{-}
,
B^{-}:=B/I^{+}
, and
B^{0}:=B/I^{0}=B_{0}/\sum_{i>0}B_{-i}B_{i}
. Then
\Spec B^{+}
is the attractor,
\Spec B^{-}
is the repeller, and
\Spec B^{0}=(\Spec B)^{\mathbb{G}_{m}}
is the fixed point space of the
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-scheme
\Spec B

[
Ric19
, §1]
. We have a commutative diagram of maps of graded
A
-algebras

\textstyle{B_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B_{\geq 0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B^{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B^{+}}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

where the vertical and the lower horizontal maps are surjective and the maps
B^{0}\to B^{+}
and
B\to B^{+}
correspond to the above defined maps
X^{+}\to X^{0}
and
X^{+}\to X
. We have a similar description with
(-)^{-}
instead of
(-)^{+}
and
B_{\leq 0}
instead of
B_{\geq 0}
.

If
X
is a group algebraic space and
\mathbb{G}_{m}
acts on
X
by group automorphisms, then
X^{+}
,
X^{-}
, and
X^{0}
are functors of groups. We will need the following special case explained in
[
CGP15
, 2.1]

Remark 1.5
.

Let
A
be a ring, let
G
be an affine group scheme over
A
and let

(1.2.3)

\mu\colon\mathbb{G}_{m,A}\times G\longrightarrow G

be a
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action by group automorphisms, in other words,
\mu
is a cocharacter of
\underline{\Aut}(G)
. For instance, if
\mu
is cocharacter of
G
, then we obtain
\mu
as above by composing
\mu
with
G\to\underline{\Aut}(G)
given by conjugation.

For a general
\mu
as in (
1.2.3
) we set

P^{\pm}(\mu):=G^{\pm},\qquad G^{0}=P^{+}(\mu)\cap P^{-}(\mu)=\Cent_{G}(\mu):=G^{\mu(\mathbb{G}_{m})},

and these are closed
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-invariant subgroup schemes of
G
whose formation is compatible with arbitary base change
\Spec A^{\prime}\to\Spec A
. One has
P^{\pm}(\mu^{-1})=P^{\mp}(\mu)
. The canonical
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-equivariant maps
P^{\pm}(\mu)\longrightarrow\Cent_{G}(\mu)
are surjective homomorphisms of group schemes. We set

U^{\pm}(\mu):=\Ker(P^{\pm}(\mu)\to\Cent_{G}(\mu)).

The multiplication map yields an isomorphism of group schemes
1
1

1

In
[
CGP15
, 2.1.8]
, this is only formulated if
G
is of finite presentation and if
\mu
is given by a cocharacter of
G
, but the proof in loc. cit. shows that this hypothesis is superfluous.

\Cent_{G}(\mu)\rtimes U^{\pm}(\mu)\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}P^{\pm}(\mu)

If
G
is of finite presentation (resp. smooth) over
A
, then
P^{\pm}(\mu)
,
U^{\pm}(\mu)
, and
\Cent_{G}(\mu)
are of finite presentation (resp. smooth) over
A
.

If
G
is a reductive group scheme over
A
, then the group scheme of outer automorphisms of
G
is étale locally constant and hence every
\mu
is given étale locally by a cocharacter of
G
. Then
P^{+}(\mu)
and
P^{-}(\mu)
are parabolic subgroups of
G
with unipotent radicals
U^{+}(\mu)
and
U^{-}(\mu)
and common Levi subgroup
\Cent_{G}(\mu)
.

1.3.
Quasi-coherent modules and vector bundles over quotient stacks by
\mathbb{G}_{m}

We continue to denote by
A
a ring and by
B
a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
A
-algebra. Set
X=\Spec B
,
{\mathcal{X}}=[\mathbb{G}_{m,A}\backslash X]
, and let
\pi\colon X\to{\mathcal{X}}
be the canonical map.

Quasi-coherent modules on
{\mathcal{X}}
.

The abelian category of quasi-coherent
{\mathscr{O}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
-modules is equivalent to the category of
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
B
-modules, where maps are given by homogeneous
B
-linear maps of degree
0
: If
{\mathscr{M}}
is a quasi-coherent
{\mathscr{O}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
-module,
\pi^{*}{\mathscr{E}}
is a quasi-coherent module over
X=\Spec B
and hence corresponds to a
B
-module
M
. By descent, this yields an equivalence between quasi-coherent
{\mathscr{O}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
-modules
{\mathscr{M}}
and
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-equivariant
B
-modules, i.e., with
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
B
-modules.

This equivalence respects tensor products and hence is an equivalence of symmetric monoidal abelian categories. Here the tensor product of two graded
B
-modules
M
and
N
is the graded
B
-module whose underlying module is the tensor product
M\otimes_{B}N
obtained by forgetting the graduations. It is endowed with the
\mathbb{Z}
-grading such that
(M\otimes_{B}N)_{d}
is generated by
m\otimes n
with
m\in M_{k}
,
n\in N_{l}
such that
k+l=d
.

Since
\pi
is affine,
\pi_{*}
is exact on quasi-coherent modules. If
M
is a
B
-module corresponding to a quasi-coherent
{\mathscr{O}}_{X}
-module
{\mathscr{M}}
, then
\pi_{*}{\mathscr{M}}
is the quasi-coherent module corresponding to the graded
B
-module
M
with
M=M_{0}
.

Let
f\colon{\mathcal{X}}\to\Spec B_{0}
be the structure morphism, then this is a good moduli space in the sense of
[
Alp13
]
. If
{\mathscr{M}}
is a quasi-coherent
{\mathscr{O}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
-module corresponding to a graded
B
-module
M
, then
f_{*}{\mathscr{M}}
is the quasi-coherent module corresponding to the
B_{0}
-module
M_{0}
. Conversely, if
{\mathscr{N}}
is a quasi-coherent module over
\Spec B_{0}
corresponding to a
B_{0}
-module
N
, then
f^{*}{\mathscr{N}}
corresponds to the
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
B
-module
M
with
M_{0}=N
and
M_{j}=0
for
j\neq 0
.

By descent for
\pi
, a quasi-coherent
{\mathscr{O}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
-module
{\mathscr{M}}
is of finite type (resp. of finite presentation, resp. flat, resp. a vector bundle) if and only if the underlying
B
-module of the corresponding
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
B
-module
M
has the same property.

Twisted line bundles.

For
e\in\mathbb{Z}
we denote by
B(e)
the
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
B
-module defined by
B
with its shifted grading, i.e.,
B(e)_{d}=B_{e+d}
. It corresponds to a line bundle on
{\mathcal{X}}
that we denote
{\mathscr{O}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}(e)
. For every quasi-coherent
{\mathscr{O}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
-module
{\mathscr{M}}
we set
{\mathscr{M}}(e):={\mathscr{M}}\otimes_{{\mathscr{O}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}}{\mathscr{O}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}(e)
. If
{\mathscr{M}}
corresponds to the graded
B
-module
M
, then
{\mathscr{M}}(e)
corresponds to the graded
B
-module
M(e)
with
M(e)_{d}=M_{d+e}
.

If
{\mathscr{M}}
and
{\mathscr{N}}
are quasi-coherent
{\mathscr{O}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
-modules given by
\mathbb{Z}
-graded modules
M
and
N
, respectively, then
\Hom_{{\mathscr{O}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}}({\mathscr{M}},{\mathscr{N}})
is identified with the set of
B
-linear map
M\to N
of degree
0
. For instance, one has

(1.3.1)

\displaystyle\Gamma({\mathcal{X}},{\mathscr{N}}(e))

\displaystyle=\Hom_{{\mathscr{O}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}}({\mathscr{O}}_{{\mathcal{X}}},{\mathscr{N}}(e))

\displaystyle=\Hom_{B}(B,N(e))^{0}=N_{e},

where
\Hom_{B}(-,-)^{0}
denotes
B
-linear maps of
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
B
-modules of degree
0
.

Vector bundles and modules of finite presentation on
{\mathcal{X}}
.

A
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
B
-module is called
graded free
if it is isomorphic to a direct sum of graded
B
-modules of the type
B(e)
. It is called
finite graded free
if there exists
r\geq 0
and
e_{1},\dots,e_{r}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
M\cong\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}B(e_{i})
.

To describe vector bundles on
{\mathcal{X}}
recall that a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
B
-module
M
is called
projective
if it satisfies the following equivalent conditions.

(i)

M
is projective as an object in the abelian category of
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
B
-modules.

(ii)

M
is projective as a
B
-module.

(iii)

M
is a direct summand of a graded free
B
-module.

Therefore we have the following characterization of vector bundles.

Proposition 1.6
.

Let
{\mathscr{M}}
be a quasi-coherent
{\mathscr{O}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
-module corresponding to a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
B
-module
M
. Then the following assertions are equivalent.

(1)

{\mathscr{M}}
is a vector bundle.

(2)

{\mathscr{M}}
is flat and of finite presentation.

(3)

{\mathscr{M}}
is a direct summand of a finite graded free module, i.e. to a graded module of the form
\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}{\mathscr{O}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}(e_{i})
for some
r\geq 0
and some
e_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(4)

{\mathscr{M}}
is Zariski locally on
{\mathcal{X}}
a finite graded free module.

(5)

M
is a finite projective
B
-module.

To check whether
{\mathscr{M}}
is of finite presentation we will use the following criterion.

Proposition 1.7
.

Let
{\mathscr{M}}
be a quasi-coherent
{\mathscr{O}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
-module corresponding a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
B
-module
M
. Then the following assertions are equivalent.

(i)

{\mathscr{M}}
is of finite type (resp. of finite presentation).

(ii)

M
is of finite type (resp. of finite presentation) as a
B
-module.

(iii)

There exists an exact sequence of graded
B
-modules

F\longrightarrow M\longrightarrow 0\qquad\qquad(\text{resp.}\qquad F^{\prime}\longrightarrow F\longrightarrow M\longrightarrow 0),

where
F
and
F^{\prime}
are finite graded free
B
-modules.

Proof.

The equivalence of
(i)
and
(ii)
is clear by descent. Let us prove the equivalence of
(ii)
and
(iii)
. Since
F
and
F^{\prime}
are both finitely generated
B
-modules, it is clear that
(iii)
implies
(ii)
. Conversely, suppose that
M
is of finite type. Let
m_{1},\dots,m_{r}
be generators of
M
which we may assume to be homogeneous, say of degree
e_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then
m_{i}
defines a
B
-linear map
B(-e_{i})\to M
, sending
1\in B(-e_{i})_{e_{i}}=B_{0}
to
m_{i}
. Hence the
m_{i}
define a surjection
u\colon\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}B(-e_{i})\to M
of graded
B
-modules. If
M
is of finite presentation, we can apply the same argument to the finitely generated
B
-module
\Ker(u)
.
∎

Derived categories of quasi-coherent modules.

The category
D_{\textup{qc}}({\mathcal{X}})
is equivalent to the category of objects in
D(B)
that are endowed with a
\mathbb{Z}
-grading.

Base change.

Let
B\to C
be a map of
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
A
-algebras. Then we obtain an induced map of algebraic stacks
f\colon[\Spec C/\mathbb{G}_{m,A}]\to[\Spec B/\mathbb{G}_{m,A}]
. If
{\mathscr{E}}
is a quasi-coherent module over
[\Spec B/\mathbb{G}_{m,A}]
corresponding to a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
B
-module
M
, then
f^{*}{\mathscr{E}}
corresponds to the graded
C
-module
C\otimes_{B}M
.

We will also use the derived pullback: Let
E\in D_{\textup{qc}}([\Spec B/\mathbb{G}_{m,A}])
, considered as a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded family
(M_{i})_{i}
with
M_{i}\in D(B)
, then
Lf^{*}E\in D_{\textup{qc}}([\Spec C/\mathbb{G}_{m,A}])
corresponds to
(M_{i}\mathrel{\smash{\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}}}_{B}C)_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
.

1.4.
Colimits of quotient stacks by
\mathbb{G}_{m}

Our next goal is to consider colimits of quotient stacks. For this we first have to agree where we form the colimit. To have a purely algebraic theory and not only a theory over formal schemes, we would like to have that for an
I
-adically complete ring
A
one has
\Spec A=\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{n}\Spec A/I^{n}
. Of course, if we form
\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{n}\Spec A/I^{n}
in the category of fppf sheaves or in the
\infty
-category of all derived stacks we would obtain
\Spf A
. Hence we will form the colimit in the 2-category of Adams stacks (Definition
A.1
) studied in detail by Schäppi. He proves that there, one indeed has that
\Spec A=\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{n}\Spec A/I^{n}
:

Example 1.8
.

(
[
Sch15
, 4.3]
)
Let
A
be a ring and let
I_{0}\supseteq I_{1}\supseteq I_{2}\supseteq\dots
be a sequence of ideals in
A
such that the ideal
I_{n}/I_{n+1}
is contained in the Jacobson radical of
A/I_{n+1}
for all
n\geq 0
. Set
\hat{A}:=\lim_{n}A/I_{n}
. Then the canonical morphisms
\Spec A/I_{n}\to\Spec\hat{A}
yield an isomorphism of Adams stacks

\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{n}\Spec A/I_{n}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\Spec\hat{A}.

Let
A
be a ring and let
n\mapsto B_{n}
be an
\mathbb{N}^{\opp}
-diagram of
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
A
-algebras
B_{n}=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}(B_{n})_{i}
. Define a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
A
-algebra
B=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}B_{i}
with
B_{i}=\lim_{n}(B_{n})_{i}
where the multiplication
B_{i}\times B_{j}\to B_{i+j}
is the limit of the multiplications on the
B_{n}
. Set
{\mathcal{X}}:=[\mathbb{G}_{m,A}\backslash\Spec B]
and
{\mathcal{X}}_{n}:=[\mathbb{G}_{m,A}\backslash\Spec B_{n}]
. Note that
{\mathcal{X}}
and
{\mathcal{X}}_{n}
are indeed Adams stacks by Proposition
A.4
. We obtain maps of Adams stacks
{\mathcal{X}}_{n}\to{\mathcal{X}}_{n+1}
for all
n
and a map

\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{n}{\mathcal{X}}_{n}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{X}},

where we form the colimit in the 2-category of Adams stacks.

Proposition 1.9
.

Suppose that the following conditions are satisfied.

(a)

The maps of rings
(B_{n+1})_{i}\to(B_{n})_{i}
are surjective for all
n\geq 1
and all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(b)

The kernel of
(B_{n+1})_{0}\to(B_{n})_{0}
is contained in the Jacobson radical of
(B_{n+1})_{0}
.

(c)

Then
(B_{n})_{0}
-modules
(B_{n})_{i}
are finite projective for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Then the map of Adams stacks
\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{n}{\mathcal{X}}_{n}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{X}}
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

We apply Theorem
A.7
. Therefore it suffices to to show that

(1)

\textup{Vec}({\mathcal{X}})\longrightarrow\lim_{n}\textup{Vec}({\mathcal{X}}_{n})
is an equivalence and that

(2)

if
w\colon{\mathscr{E}}\to{\mathscr{F}}
is a map of finitely presented modules over
{\mathcal{X}}
such that its pullback to
{\mathcal{X}}_{n}
is surjective for some
n
, then
w
is surjective.

Hypotheses
(a)
and
(b)
show by Example
1.8
that
\mathop{\textup{colim}}(B_{n})_{0}=B_{n}
as Adams stacks. Hence
\textup{Vec}(B_{0})\to\lim_{n}\textup{Vec}((B_{n})_{0})
is an equivalence by the last assertion of Theorem
A.7
. By Hypothesis
(c)
this implies that

\textup{Vec}({\mathcal{X}})\longrightarrow\lim_{n}\textup{Vec}({\mathcal{X}}_{n})

is fully faithful if restricted to finite graded free modules. Since every vector bundle is a direct summand of a finite graded free module, this shows that (*) is fully faithful.

Let us show essential surjectivity. Let
({\mathscr{E}}_{n})_{n}\in\lim_{n}\textup{Vec}({\mathcal{X}}_{n})
which we consider as a compatible system
(M_{n})_{n}
of graded projective
B_{n}
-modules
M_{n}
. Choose a surjection
p_{1}\colon F_{1}\to M_{1}
, where
F_{1}
is finite graded free, say
F_{1}=\bigoplus_{s}B_{1}(e_{s})
for finitely many
e_{s}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Set
F_{n}:=\bigoplus_{s}B_{n}(e_{s})
and
F:=\bigoplus_{s}B(e_{s})
. By Hypothesis
(a)
we can lift
p_{1}
inductively to
B_{n}
-linear maps
F_{n}\to M_{n}
. These maps are surjective in every degree by Nakayamas lemma and by Hypothesis
(b)
. Since
M_{n}
is finite graded projective, we find sections
s_{n}
of
p_{n}
for all
n
.

We claim that we can arrange the
s_{n}
inductively such that
s_{n+1}
lifts
s_{n}
for all
n
. Indeed, let
\bar{s}_{n+1}
the reduction of
s_{n+1}
and consider the composition

M_{n+1}\to M_{n}\mathrel{\smash{\overset{s_{n}-\bar{s}_{n+1}}{\mathrel{\hbox to50.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}}}}\Ker(p_{n})

Since
\Ker(p_{n+1})\to\Ker(p_{n})
is surjective by the snake lemma and since
M_{n+1}
is graded projective, we can lift the composition to a homomorphism
t_{n+1}\colon M_{n+1}\to\Ker(p_{n+1})
and we can replace
s_{n+1}
by
s_{n+1}+t_{n+1}
which proves the claim.

Hence we obtain a compatible family of idempotent endomorphisms
e_{n}:=s_{n}\circ p_{n}
of
F_{n}
with
M_{n}=\mathop{\rm Im}(e_{n})
which defines an idempotent endomorphism
e
of
F
, and
\mathop{\rm Im}(e)
defines a vector bundle on
{\mathcal{X}}
whose reduction to
{\mathcal{X}}_{n}
is
{\mathscr{E}}_{n}
for all
n
. This shows
(1)
.

To prove
(2)
, it suffices to show that
w
induces a surjective map
M_{j}\to N_{j}
for all
j
where
M=\bigoplus M_{j}
corresponds to
{\mathscr{E}}
and
N=\bigoplus N_{j}
to
{\mathscr{F}}
. Since
{\mathscr{F}}
is of finite presentation,
N
is the cokernel of a map of finite graded free modules. By
(c)
this implies that each
N_{j}
is a cokernel of a map of finite projective modules and hence of finite presentation. Therefore
(2)
follows from Nakayama’s lemma.
∎

Now suppose that
A
is given the structure of an
O
-algebra, where
O
is a Dedekind domain, and let
G
be a flat affine group scheme of finite type over
O
. Then the classifying stack
BG
is an Adams stack by Example
A.5
.

Corollary 1.10
.

Under the hypothesis of Proposition
1.9
, there is a canonical isomorphism of groupoids

\Bun_{G}({\mathcal{X}})\overset{\sim}{\to}\lim_{n}\Bun_{G}({\mathcal{X}}_{n}).

Proof.

By definition we have

\displaystyle\Bun_{G}({\mathcal{X}})

\displaystyle=\Hom_{O}({\mathcal{X}},BG)

\displaystyle=\Hom_{O}(\mathop{\textup{colim}}{\mathcal{X}}_{n},BG)

\displaystyle=\lim_{n}\Hom_{O}({\mathcal{X}}_{n},BG)

\displaystyle=\lim_{n}\Bun_{G}({\mathcal{X}}_{n}),

where the second equality holds by Proposition
1.9
.
∎

2.
Filtered rings and their Rees stacks

2.1.
Filtered modules over rings

Let
A
be a ring. Let
A[t]
be the polynomial ring with the
\mathbb{Z}
-grading given by
\deg(t)=-1
, let
(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{A})^{-}
be the corresponding
\mathbb{G}_{m,A}
-scheme, and set

(2.1.1)

A^{\Fil}:=[\mathbb{G}_{m,A}\backslash(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{A})^{-}].

To clarify the notation let us remark that here and in the following lines,
\mathbb{G}_{m,A}\backslash
always denotes a quotient by a
\mathbb{G}_{m,A}
-action whereas all other
\setminus
-signs denote complements. The algebraic stack
A^{\Fil}
has a stratification given by the open affine immersion

(2.1.2)

\Spec A=[\mathbb{G}_{m,A}\backslash(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{A}\setminus\{0\})]\longrightarrow A^{\Fil}

induced by the inclusion
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{A}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{A}
and by the closed regular immersion of codimension 1

(2.1.3)

B\mathbb{G}_{m,A}=[\mathbb{G}_{m,A}\backslash\{0\}]\longrightarrow A^{\Fil}.

Let
\varphi\colon A\to B
be a map of rings. It induces a map of algebraic stacks

(2.1.4)

\varphi^{\Fil}\colon B^{\Fil}\to A^{\Fil},

and the diagram

\textstyle{B^{\Fil}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{\Fil}}
\textstyle{A^{\Fil}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Spec B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Spec\varphi}
\textstyle{\Spec A}

is cartesian with faithfully flat vertical maps of finite presentation.

Definition 2.1
.

A
filtered
A
-module
is a quasi-coherent
{\mathscr{O}}_{A^{\Fil}}
-module. A
map of filtered
A
-modules
is a homomorphism of quasi-coherent
{\mathscr{O}}_{A^{\Fil}}
-modules.

We obtain the abelian category of filtered
A
-modules. Let us make this definition more explicit. By Section
1.3
a quasi-coherent
{\mathscr{O}}_{A^{\Fil}}
-module
{\mathscr{M}}
is given by a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
A[t]
-module
M
which can be described as a chain of
A
-modules

\cdots\mathrel{\smash{\overset{t}{\longrightarrow}}}M_{j+1}\mathrel{\smash{\overset{t}{\longrightarrow}}}M_{j}\mathrel{\smash{\overset{t}{\longrightarrow}}}M_{j-1}\mathrel{\smash{\overset{t}{\longrightarrow}}}\cdots

with
A
-linear maps
t\colon M_{j}\to M_{j-1}
. The underlying
A[t]
-module of
M
corresponds to the quasi-coherent module obtained from
{\mathscr{M}}
by pullback via the canonical map
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{A}\to A^{\Fil}
. Note that the maps
t\colon M_{j}\to M_{j-1}
are not necessarily injective. A map of filtered
A
-modules is then a map of
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
A[t]
-modules.

In the sequel we will usually use notations like
{\mathscr{M}}
,
{\mathscr{E}}
,
{\mathscr{B}}
etc. to denote the quasi-coherent
{\mathscr{O}}_{A^{\Fil}}
-module and
M
,
E
,
B
etc. to denote the corresponding
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
A[t]
-module.

Occasionally we will also work with objects in the derived category
D_{\textup{qc}}(A^{\Fil})
. It can be identified with filtered derived category of
A
by
[
Mou21
]
.

Remark 2.2
.

By definition, all filtrations are descending. Of course one can also work with increasing filtrations by considering quasi-coherent modules over
[\mathbb{G}_{m}\backslash(\mathbb{A}^{1})^{+}]
, where
\mathbb{G}_{m}
acts on
\mathbb{A}^{1}
with the standard action.

More generally, let
L
be a line bundle on
A
and consider
\Sym_{A}(L)=\bigoplus_{j\geq 0}L^{\otimes j}
. Then a quasi-coherent module over the quotient stack
[\mathbb{G}_{m,A}\backslash\Sym_{A}(L)]
is a family
(M_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
of
A
-modules together with
A
-linear maps
M_{j}\otimes L\to M_{j+1}
for all
j
.

The underlying module.

We can write
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{A}\setminus\{0\}=\Spec A[t,t^{-1}]
with

A[t,t^{-1}]=\mathop{\textup{colim}}(A[t]\mathrel{\smash{\overset{t}{\longrightarrow}}}A[t]\mathrel{\smash{\overset{t}{\longrightarrow}}}\cdots).

Therefore pullback along the open immersion (
2.1.2
) sends a quasi-coherent
{\mathscr{O}}_{A^{\Fil}}
-module
{\mathscr{M}}
corresponding to
M=(M_{j}\mathrel{\smash{\overset{t}{\longrightarrow}}}M_{j-1})_{j}
to the
A
-module

M_{-\infty}:=\mathop{\textup{colim}}(\cdots\mathrel{\smash{\overset{t}{\longrightarrow}}}M_{j+1}\mathrel{\smash{\overset{t}{\longrightarrow}}}M_{j}\mathrel{\smash{\overset{t}{\longrightarrow}}}M_{j-1}\mathrel{\smash{\overset{t}{\longrightarrow}}}\cdots),

which we call the
underlying
A
-module
of the filtered
A
-module
M
or of
{\mathscr{M}}
. It should not be confused with the
A
-module underlying the
A[t]
-module
M=\bigoplus_{j}M_{j}
. Occasionally, we will also denote a filtered module by
(M_{\infty},\Fil^{j})
with
\Fil^{j}=M^{j}
, in particular if the maps
M_{j}\to M_{j-1}
are all injective, e.g., in the case of vector bundles (see Proposition
2.4
below).

More generally, for
{\mathscr{M}}\in D_{\textup{qc}}(A^{\Fil})
considered as an object
(\cdots\to M_{j}\to M_{j-1}\to\cdots)
in the filtered derived category of
A
-modules, its pullback via
\Spec A\to A^{\Fil}
is the element
\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{j\to-\infty}M_{j}
of
D(A)
.

The graded module attached to a filtered module.

Now consider the closed regular immersion
i\colon B\mathbb{G}_{m,A}\longrightarrow A^{\Fil}
of codimension
1
. Let
{\mathscr{M}}
be in
D_{\textup{qc}}(A^{\Fil})
corresponding to an object
M=(\cdots\to M_{j}\to M_{j-1}\to\cdots)
in the filtered derived category of
A
-modules. Then

\gr(M):=Li^{*}{\mathscr{M}}

is called the
graded module attached to
M
. It is an object in
D_{\textup{qc}}(B\mathbb{G}_{m,A})
which we can also consider as a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded object in
D(A)
.

If
{\mathscr{M}}
is a filtered
A
-module given by
M=(\cdots\to M_{j}\to M_{j-1}\to\cdots)
, then
\gr(M)
is given by

(2.1.5)

\gr(M)=\bigoplus_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}(M_{j+1}\mathrel{\smash{\overset{t}{\longrightarrow}}}M_{j}),

where we consider
M_{j+1}\mathrel{\smash{\overset{t}{\longrightarrow}}}M_{j}
as a complex in
D(A)
concentrated in degree
-1
and
0
:
We have
A=A[t]/(t)\cong(A[t]\mathrel{\smash{\overset{t}{\longrightarrow}}}A[t])
in the derived category
D(A[t])
.

In particular we find

(2.1.6)

\Tor^{A[t]}_{1}(A,M)=H^{-1}(\gr(M))=\bigoplus_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\Ker(M_{j+1}\mathrel{\smash{\overset{t}{\longrightarrow}}}M_{j}).

This also shows that
t\colon M\to M
is injective if and only if
\Tor^{A[t]}_{1}(A,M)=0
. In this case one has
Li^{*}{\mathscr{E}}=i^{*}{\mathscr{E}}
which corresponds to the graded
\mathbb{Z}
-module
\bigoplus_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\Coker(M_{j+1}\mathrel{\smash{\overset{t}{\longrightarrow}}}M_{j})
. This is for instance the case if
{\mathscr{M}}
is a flat
{\mathscr{O}}_{A^{\Fil}}
-module or, equivalently, if
M
is a flat
A[t]
-module.

Definition and Remark 2.3
.

A filtered
A
-module
M
is called
positively graded
if
\gr(M)_{j}=0
for all
j<0
, in other words by (
2.1.5
), if
t\colon M_{j+1}\to M_{j}
is an isomorphism for all
j<0
.

Filtered vector bundles.

Proposition 2.4
.

A quasi-coherent
{\mathscr{O}}_{A^{\Fil}}
-module
{\mathscr{M}}
corresponding to
(\dots\to M_{j}\mathrel{\smash{\overset{t}{\longrightarrow}}}M_{j-1}\to\dots)
is a vector bundle over
A^{\Fil}
if and only if the following conditions are satisfied

(a)

M_{j}
is finite projective for all
j
,

(b)

t\colon M_{j}\to M_{j-1}
is injective with finite projective cokernel for all
j
,

(c)

One has
M_{j}=0
for
j\gg 0
, and
t\colon M_{j}\to M_{j-1}
is an isomorphism for
j\ll 0
.

Proof.

Since every vector bundle corresponds to a direct summand of a finite graded free
A[t]
-module, the conditions are clearly necessary. Conversely, we have to show that the conditions imply that
M=\bigoplus_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}M_{j}
is a finite projective
A[t]
-module. For this, we apply Proposition
B.1
to
I=(t)
. Since
(c)
holds and
M_{j}
is of finite presentation for all
j
,
M
is an
A[t]
-module of finite presentation by Proposition
1.7
. Moreover,
(a)
and
(c)
imply that the
A
-module
M_{-\infty}
is finite projective, therefore
M[1/t]=M_{-\infty}\otimes_{A}A[t,t^{-1}]
is a finite projective
A[t,t^{-1}]
-module. Hence
(b)
implies that Condition
(a)
of Proposition
B.1
is satisfied by (
2.1.6
).
∎

Therefore the underlying module of a filtered vector bundle is again a vector bundle which can be identified with
M_{j}
for
j\ll 0
. We obtain the classical version of a filtered vector bundle as, for instance, defined in
[
Zie15
]
. The attached graded module is the graded vector bundle
\bigoplus_{j}M_{j-1}/t(M_{j})
.

Remark and Definition 2.5
.

Let
L
be a line bundle over
A
. Then working Zariski locally one sees that a vector bundle over
[\mathbb{G}_{m,A}\backslash\Sym_{A}(L)]
is a family
(N_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
of finite projective
A
-modules together with
A
-linear maps
N_{j}\otimes_{A}L\to N_{j+1}
for all
j
such that
N_{j}=0
for
j\ll 0
,
N_{j}\otimes_{A}L\to N_{j+1}
is an isomorphism for
j\gg 0
, and
N_{j}\otimes_{A}L\to N_{j+1}
is injective with projective cokernel for all
j
.

We call such a datum a
vector bundle with an (increasing)
L
-filtration
.

Pullback of filtered modules.

Let
\psi\colon A\to C
be a homomorphism of rings yielding a map
\psi^{\Fil}\colon C^{\Fil}\to A^{\Fil}
(
2.1.4
). If
{\mathscr{M}}\in D_{\textup{qc}}(A^{\Fil})
is a filtered object of
D(A)
, we obtain the derived pullback
L\varphi^{\Fil*}{\mathscr{M}}
. Suppose that
{\mathscr{M}}
corresponds to
(M_{j}\mathrel{\smash{\overset{t}{\longrightarrow}}}M_{j-1})_{j}
with
M_{j}\in D(A)
, then
L\varphi^{\Fil*}{\mathscr{M}}
corresponds to
(M_{j}\mathrel{\smash{\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}}}_{A}B\mathrel{\smash{\overset{t}{\longrightarrow}}}M_{j-1}\mathrel{\smash{\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}}}_{A}B)_{j}
.

If
{\mathscr{M}}
is a flat quasi-coherent module over
A^{\Fil}
(e.g., if
{\mathscr{M}}
is a vector bundle over
A^{\Fil}
) or if
\varphi
is flat, equivalently if
\varphi^{\Fil}
is flat, then
L\varphi^{\Fil*}{\mathscr{M}}=\varphi^{\Fil*}{\mathscr{M}}
.

2.2.
Filtered rings and their Rees stacks

Again let
A
be a ring and define
A^{\Fil}
as in (
2.1.1
).

Definition 2.6
.

(1)

A
filtered
A
-algebra
is a quasi-coherent
{\mathscr{O}}_{A^{\Fil}}
-algebra. A
map of filtered
A
-algebras
is a map of quasi-coherent
{\mathscr{O}}_{A^{\Fil}}
-algebras.

(2)

A filtered
A
-algebra is called
positively graded
if it is positively graded as a filtered module.

A filtered
A
-algebra
{\mathscr{B}}
is given by a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
A
-algebra
B=\bigoplus B_{j}
together with
A
-linear maps
t\colon B_{j}\to B_{j-1}
for all
j\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

t(xy)=t(x)y=xt(y),\qquad\qquad\text{for $x\in B_{j}$, $y\in B_{i}$}.

As above one sees that if
{\mathscr{B}}
is flat as a
A^{\Fil}
-algebra, then
t\colon B\to B
is injective.

Definition and Remark 2.7
.

A
filtration on
A
is a filtered positively graded
A
-algebra with corresponding
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
A
-algebra
B
such that the canonical map
A\to B_{0}
is an isomorphism.

A filtration on
A
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
A
-algebra
B=\bigoplus_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}B_{j}
together with
A
-linear maps

\cdots\mathrel{\smash{\overset{t}{\longrightarrow}}}B_{j+1}\mathrel{\smash{\overset{t}{\longrightarrow}}}B_{j}\mathrel{\smash{\overset{t}{\longrightarrow}}}\cdots\mathrel{\smash{\overset{t}{\longrightarrow}}}B_{0}=A\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}B_{-1}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}B_{-2}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\cdots.

In this case, we also write usually
\Fil^{j}
instead of
B_{j}
and call
(A,(\Fil^{j})_{j\geq 0})
a
filtered ring
. By definition we have an isomorphism
A[t]\overset{\sim}{\to}\bigoplus_{j\leq 0}B_{j}
and we usually identify
B_{j}
with
A
for
j\leq 0
.

Remark and Definition 2.8
.

A quasi-coherent
{\mathscr{O}}_{A^{\Fil}}
-algebra
{\mathscr{B}}
corresponds to an affine map of stacks

(2.2.1)

\Spec{\mathscr{B}}\longrightarrow A^{\Fil}.

If
B
is the
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
A[t]
-algebra corresponding to
{\mathscr{B}}
we call

\Re(B):=\Spec{\mathscr{B}}=[\mathbb{G}_{m,A}\backslash\Spec B]

the
Rees stack attached to the filtered
A
-algebra
B
. If
B
is a filtered ring
(A,(\Fil^{j}))
, then we also write
\Re(A,(\Fil^{j}))
or even
\Re(A)
if there can’t be any confusion about the filtration.

Remark and Definition 2.9
.

Pulling back along the open immersion
\Spec A\to A^{\Fil}
one obtains for every filtered
A
-algebra
B=(t\colon B_{j}\to B_{j-1})
the
underlying
A
-algebra

B_{-\infty}=\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{t}B_{j}
, i.e., we have a cartesian diagram

(2.2.2)

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 22.98845pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-22.98845pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Spec B_{-\infty}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 48.05652pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-26.20337pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 48.05652pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Re(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 62.59993pt\raise-22.46332pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-17.1117pt\raise-33.53668pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Spec A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 46.98845pt\raise-33.53668pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 46.98845pt\raise-33.53668pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A^{\Fil}.}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

If
(A,(\Fil^{i}))
is a filtered ring, its underlying ring is
A=B_{0}\cong B_{-\infty}
.

For general filtered
A
-algebras, the notion of underlying
A
-algebra can be a bit counterintuitive, see the next Example
2.11
for a non-trivial filtered
A
-algebra whose underlying
A
-algebra is the zero ring.

Example 2.10
.

Let
B_{-\infty}
be any
A
-algebra, which we consider as quasi-coherent algebra
{\mathscr{B}}_{-\infty}
over
\Spec A
. Then pushforward along the affine open immersion
\nu\colon\Spec A\to A^{\Fil}
defines a filtered
A
-algebra given by the
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
A[t]
-algebra
B_{-\infty}[t,t^{-1}]
.

If
{\mathscr{B}}
is any filtered
A
-algebra corresponding to
B=(t\colon B_{j}\to B_{j-1})
, then the canonical map
{\mathscr{B}}\to\nu_{*}\nu^{*}{\mathscr{B}}
defines a map of filtered
A
-algebras

B\longrightarrow B_{-\infty}[t,t^{-1}]

which in degree
j\in\mathbb{Z}
is given by the natural map
B_{j}\to B_{-\infty}
. The map (*) corresponds to a map of the associated Rees stacks

(2.2.3)

\tau\colon\Re(B_{-\infty}[t,t^{-1}])=\Spec B_{-\infty}\longrightarrow\Re(B)

whose composition with
\Re(B)\to A^{\Fil}
is the same as the composition
\Spec B_{-\infty}\to\Spec A\to A^{\Fil}
.

If
{\mathscr{B}}
defines a filtration
(\Fil^{j})
on
A
, then
B_{-\infty}=A
and (
2.2.3
) is a morphism of stacks over
A^{\Fil}

(2.2.4)

\tau\colon\Spec A\longrightarrow\Re(A,(\Fil^{j})).

For a different way to describe
\tau
in this case, see also Remark
2.13
below.

Example 2.11
.

Let
v\colon\Spec A\to A^{\Fil}
be a section of the structure map
A^{\Fil}\to\Spec A
. It corresponds to a line bundle
L
on
A
and an
A
-linear map
v\colon L\to A
(Example
1.2
).

Define a filtered
A
-algebra
B
by
B_{j}:=L^{\otimes j}
for
j\in\mathbb{Z}
where
t\colon B_{j}\to B_{j-1}
is given by multiplication with
v
. Its underlying
\mathbb{Z}
-graded algebra is
T(L)
(Remark
1.1
) and we denote this filtered
A
-algebra by
T(v)
. One has

\Re(T(v))=[\mathbb{G}_{m}\backslash\Spec T(L)]=\Spec A

and the canonical map
\Spec A\to A^{\Fil}
is just
v
. Its underlying
A
-algebra is

B_{-\infty}=A[1/v]:=\mathop{\textup{colim}}(\cdots\mathrel{\smash{\overset{v}{\longrightarrow}}}L^{\otimes j+1}\mathrel{\smash{\overset{v}{\longrightarrow}}}L^{\otimes j}\mathrel{\smash{\overset{v}{\longrightarrow}}}\cdots).

Here the multiplication of two elements
c,c^{\prime}\in A[1/v]
represented by
\tilde{c}\in L^{\otimes j}
and
\tilde{c}^{\prime}\in L^{\otimes j^{\prime}}
is defined as the image of
\tilde{c}\otimes\tilde{c}^{\prime}\in L^{\otimes j+j^{\prime}}
in
A[1/v]
. Hence
\Spec A[1/v]
is the largest open subscheme of
\Spec A
on which
v
is surjective and hence an isomorphism.

If
I=L
is an invertible ideal of
A
and
v
is the inclusion, we also write
T(I)
instead of
T(v)
and
A[1/I]
instead of
A[1/v]
.
For instance, if
I=(z)
for a regular element
z\in A
and
v
is the inclusion, then
B_{-\infty}=A[1/z]
.

Another example is
v=0
. In that case, the canonical map
\Spec A\to A^{\Fil}
is the composition

0\colon\Spec A\longrightarrow B\mathbb{G}_{m,A}\mathrel{\smash{\overset{i}{\longrightarrow}}}A^{\Fil}.

Its underlying
A
-algebra is the zero ring
A[1/0]
.

Remark 2.12

(Points of the Rees stack)
.

Let
B=(B_{j+1}\mathrel{\smash{\overset{t}{\longrightarrow}}}B_{j})_{j}
be a filtered
A
-algebra and let
C
be an
A
-algebra. Then by Remark
1.1
, the groupoid
\Re(B)(C)
is the groupoid of triples
(L,v,\alpha)
, where

(a)

L
is a line bundle on
C
and
v\colon L\to C
is a
C
-linear map,

(b)

\alpha\colon B\longrightarrow T(v)
is a map of filtered
A
-algebras.

Let us describe attractor, repeller and fixed locus in Rees stacks of filtered rings.

Remark and Definition 2.13
.

Let
(A,(\Fil^{j}))
be a filtered ring, i.e., it is given by

\cdots\mathrel{\smash{\overset{t}{\longrightarrow}}}\Fil^{j+1}\mathrel{\smash{\overset{t}{\longrightarrow}}}\Fil^{j}\mathrel{\smash{\overset{t}{\longrightarrow}}}\cdots\mathrel{\smash{\overset{t}{\longrightarrow}}}\Fil^{0}=A\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}A\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}A\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\cdots

and
\Fil^{j}=A
for
j<0
. Let
B=\bigoplus_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\Fil^{j}
be the associated
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
A
-algebra. By (
1.2.1
) and (
1.2.2
) the ideals defining the attractor, repeller, and fixed locus of
\Spec B
are given by the
\mathbb{Z}
-graded ideals

(2.2.5)

\displaystyle I^{-}

\displaystyle=tB=tA[t]\oplus\bigoplus_{j\geq 0}t(\Fil^{j+1}),

\displaystyle I^{+}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{j\leq 0}t^{-j+1}(\Fil^{1})\oplus\bigoplus_{j>0}\Fil^{j},

\displaystyle I^{0}

\displaystyle=I^{+}+I^{-}=t(\Fil^{1})\oplus\bigoplus_{j\neq 0}\Fil^{j}.

Hence we obtain

(2.2.6)

\displaystyle B^{+}=B/I^{-}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{j\geq 0}\Fil^{j}/t(\Fil^{j+1}),

\displaystyle B^{-}=B/I^{+}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{j\leq 0}A/t^{-j+1}(\Fil^{1})=(A/t(\Fil^{1}))[t],

\displaystyle B^{0}=B/I^{0}

\displaystyle=A/t(\Fil^{1}).

For
?\in\{+,-,0\}
we denote by

\Re(B)^{?}:=[\mathbb{G}_{m,A}\backslash(\Spec B^{?})]

the corresponding quotient stacks. Then we have

(2.2.7)

\Re(B)^{0}=B\mathbb{G}_{m,B^{0}},\qquad\qquad\Re(B)^{-}=(A/t\Fil^{1})^{\Fil}

and obtain a cartesian diagram of closed immersions

(2.2.8)

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Re(B),}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-64.87114pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern
