\displaystyle=\lim_{N<\infty}\underline{\mathcal{B}\textup{un}}^{[\mu]}_{G}(\Disp,{N})

The same arguments as in the equi-characteristic shtuka case show that one has the following description of
E_{N}^{W}(G,\mu)
if
[\mu]
is represented by a cocharacter
\mu
. Let
R
be a bounded
p
-complete ring such that
W(R)
is
p
-torsion free. This implies that
G(W(R))\to G(W(R)[1/p])
is injective. Then

(7.2.1)

E^{W}_{\infty}(G,\mu)(R)=L^{W,+}G(R)\cap\mu^{-1}L^{+,W}G(R)\mu\subseteq L^{W}G(R).

If
R
is in addition an
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-algebra, then one has for
1\leq N<\infty
that

(7.2.2)

E_{N}^{W}(G,\mu)(R)=E^{W}_{\infty}(G,\mu)(R)/(L^{W}_{N}G(R)\cap\mu^{-1}L^{W}_{N}G(R)\mu),

where
L^{W}_{N}G:=\Ker(L^{W,+}G\to L^{W,(N)}G)
.

For a characterization of rings
R
such that
W(R)
is
p
-torsion free, see
[
MB25
, 4.1]
. All reduced rings
R
have this property. In characteristic
p
, also the converse holds.

Proposition 7.11
.

Let
1\leq N<\infty
. Then the canonical map

E_{N+1}^{W}(G,\mu)\to E_{N}^{W}(G,\mu)

is a surjective homomorphism of smooth algebraic groups. Its kernel is a vector group of dimension
\dim G
.

As in the equi-characteristc shtuka case (Corollary
6.24
and Proposition
6.29
) one deduces via Theorem
7.9
the following results.

Corollary 7.12
.

For every strictly henselian local
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-algebra
R
and for every
1\leq N\leq\infty
, one has an equivalence of groupoids

\underline{\mathcal{B}\textup{un}}^{[\mu]}_{G}(\Disp,{N})(R)\cong[E_{N}^{W}(G,\mu)(R)\backslash G(W_{N}(R))].

Corollary 7.13
.

For all
\infty\geq N^{\prime}>N\geq 1
and for every
\kappa
-algebra
R
, the truncation map
\underline{\mathcal{B}\textup{un}}_{G}^{[\mu]}(\Disp,N^{\prime})(R)\longrightarrow\underline{\mathcal{B}\textup{un}}_{G}^{\mu}(\Disp,N)(R)
is locally for the étale topology on
R
essentially surjective.

Now the same proof as for Theorem
6.31
, replacing
k\mathopen{[\![}z\mathclose{]\!]}
by
W(k)
, shows the existence of Traverso bounds for truncated
G
-display of type
\mu
:

Theorem 7.14
.

Let
G
be reductive over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and let
[\mu]
be a conjugacy class of cocharacter of
G
, defined over a finite étale dvr extension
O_{[\mu]}
of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. Let
k
be an algebraically closed extension of the residue filed of
O_{[\mu]}
. Then there exists an
N_{0}<\infty
such the truncation map
\underline{\mathcal{B}\textup{un}}_{G}^{\mu}(\Disp,N^{\prime})\longrightarrow\underline{\mathcal{B}\textup{un}}_{G}^{\mu}(\Disp,N)
is bijective on isomorphism classes of
k
-valued points for every
\infty\geq N^{\prime}\geq N\geq N_{0}
.

8.
Prismatic displays, prismatic Breuil–Kisin modules, and prismatic
F
-gauges

In this section, let
\underline{A}:=(A,\delta,I)
be a bounded prism and let
\phi\colon A\to A
be the attached Frobenius endomorphism.

We attach two frames to
\underline{A}
which we call the prismatic frame and the Nygaard frame. First, we will study the prismatic frame. Most results in these two sections are rephrasements of results of Kazuhiro Ito
[
Ito24
]
and
[
Ito25
]
or slight generalizations thereof in our language using the principle of geometrization.

Moreover, we will study truncations in this setting only in a later paper.

8.1.
Prismatic displays and prismatic Breuil–Kisin modules

8.1
.

The prismatic frame.

We can attach a frame to every bounded prism as follows. Let
\Re(A,I)
be the attached Rees stack defined in Section
5
, see in particular Remark
5.1
. It contains the open substacks
\Re(A,I)^{\neq-}
and
\Re(A,I)^{\neq+}
, which are both isomorphic to
\Spec A
and whose intersection is
\Spec A[1/I]
. Their union is the open substack
\Re(A,I)^{\neq 0}
, which is a non-separated scheme carrying an ample pair of line bundles (Remark
5.3
).

We define
\sigma
as in (
5.4.1
). This indeed recovers the description in
[
Ito24
, 5.2.3]
if one can and does choose a generator
d
of
I
: Then one has an isomorphism of
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
A
-algebras
T(I)\overset{\sim}{\to}T(A)
, given in degree
i
by
a\mapsto a/d^{i}
, which induces after taking quotients by
\mathbb{G}_{m}
the identity
\Spec A\overset{\sim}{\to}\Spec A
. Therefore, we
\sigma
is given by

\sigma_{d}^{*}\colon\phi^{*}\Rees(A,I)\longrightarrow T(A),

where
\sigma^{*}_{d,i}
is given for
i\geq 0
by the
\phi
-linear map
I^{i}\to A
,
a\mapsto\phi(a/d^{i})
.

We obtain a frame stack
{\mathcal{F}}(\underline{A})
, and its open substack
{\mathcal{F}}(\underline{A})^{\neq 0}
(Definition
4.6
), which we denote by

\underline{A}^{\BK}\subset\underline{A}^{\Disp}.

We call the frame stack
\underline{A}^{\Disp}
the
prism display stack
of
\underline{A}
and its open substack
\underline{A}^{\BK}
the
Breuil-Kisin stack
of
\underline{A}
.
If
\underline{A}
can be oriented by a generator
d\in I
, the underlying Rees algebra is isomorphic to
A[t,u]/(tu-d)
(Remark
5.1

(2)
).

Remark 8.2
.

Let
f\colon\underline{A}=(A,I,\delta)\to\underline{A}^{\prime}=(A^{\prime},I^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})
be a map of prisms. Then by functoriality (Remark
5.4
) we obtain an induced map of
\Re(A,I)\to\Re(A^{\prime},I^{\prime})
which induces maps

\underline{A}^{\Disp}\longrightarrow\underline{A}^{\prime\Disp},\qquad\underline{A}^{\BK}\longrightarrow\underline{A}^{\prime\BK}.

As a special case of Proposition
5.10
we have for every smooth affine group scheme
G
over
A
a bijection of pointed sets

(8.1.1)

H^{1}(\Re(A,I),G)\overset{\sim}{\to}H^{1}(B\mathbb{G}_{m,A/I},G),

which may be viewed as the existence of a prismatic normal decomposition.

We have the following definitions, which are reformulations of definitions due to Ito
[
Ito24
, 5.2.1]
,
[
Ito24
, 3.1.2]
.

Definition 8.3
.

Let
\underline{A}=(A,I,\delta)
be a bounded prism and let
G
be an affine flat group scheme over
A
.

(1)

A
G
-display over
\underline{A}
is a
G
-bundle over
\underline{A}^{\Disp}
.

(2)

A
Breuil-Kisin
G
-bundle over
\underline{A}
is a
G
-bundle over
\underline{A}^{\BK}
.

The following result reproves and generalizes
[
Ito24
, 5.3.8]
. It is a special case of Corollary
5.19
.

Proposition 8.4
.

Let
G
be an affine flat group scheme over a Dedekind domain
O
and let
\underline{A}
be a bounded prism such that
A
is an
O
-algebra.

(1)

Pullback via the open immersion
\underline{A}^{\BK}\longrightarrow\underline{A}^{\Disp}
yields a fully faithful functor from the groupoid of
G
-displays of
\underline{A}
to the groupoid of Breuil-Kisin
G
-bundles over
\underline{A}
.

(2)

Let
G
be in addition smooth. The essential image consists of those Breuil-Kisin
G
-bundles that have a type
[\mu]
(in the sense of Definition
3.8

(3)
).

If
\mu
is a cocharacter of
G
, then
G
-bundles on
\underline{A}^{\BK}
that are étale locally isomorphic to
{\mathscr{E}}_{\mu}
are the same as
G
-Breuil–Kisin modules of type
\mu
as defined in
[
Ito24
, 5.1.4]
.

Example 8.5
.

Let
R
be a perfect ring of characteristic
p
. Then
\underline{W(R)}:=(W(R),(p),\delta)
is a prism, where
\delta
is given by
p\delta(x)=\phi(x)-x^{p}
. Then
\underline{W(R)}^{\Disp}=R^{\Disp}
, where the right hand side is defined in (
7.1.1
). In particular a
G
-display over
\underline{W(R)}
is the same as a
G
-display over
R
.

If
R=k
is a perfect field, then we deduce from Corollary
5.19

(3)
that pullback via the open immersion
\underline{W(k)}^{\BK}\to\underline{W(k)}^{\Disp}
yields an equivalence of
G
-displays and Breuil–Kisin
G
-bundles over
\underline{W(k)}
if
G
is reductive.

8.2.
Prismatic
F
-gauges

8.6
.

The Nygaard frame.

Let
\underline{A}=(A,I,\delta)
be a bounded prism. For
i\geq 0
define the
Nygaard filtration

\Fil_{{\mathcal{N}}}^{i}:=\{\,a\in A\ ;\ \phi(a)\in I^{i}\,\}

which is a filtration by ideals of
A
. We denote by
\Rees_{{\mathcal{N}}}\underline{A}
the corresponding Rees algebra and by
\underline{A}^{{\mathcal{N}}}
the corresponding Rees stack. Using Remark
4.2
we define
\sigma\colon\Spec A\to\underline{A}^{{\mathcal{N}}}
by

\sigma^{*}\colon\phi^{*}\Rees_{{\mathcal{N}}}\underline{A}\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}I^{\otimes i},

where
\sigma^{*}_{i}\colon\phi^{*}\Fil_{{\mathcal{N}}}^{i}\to I^{i}
for
i\geq 0
is the
A
-linear map corresponding to
\phi\colon\Fil_{{\mathcal{N}}}^{i}\to I^{i}
and where
\sigma^{*}_{-1}(1\otimes t)=1\in A\subseteq I^{-1}
. We obtain a frame

(A,(\Fil^{i}_{{\mathcal{N}}}),\sigma)

which we call the
Nygaard frame
of the prism
(A,I,\delta)
. We denote the attached frame stack by

\underline{A}^{\Syn}

and call it the
syntomic frame stack
of
\underline{A}
.

Example 8.7
.

Let
R
be a quasi-regular semiperfectoid (or short qrsp) ring and let
({{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{R},I)
be the initial object in the prismatic site of
R
. By definition, there exists a perfectoid ring mapping to
R
and hence this prism is orientable, i.e.
I=(d)
for a regular element
d\in{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{R}
. Let
\varphi\colon{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{R}\to{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{R}
be its Frobenius. Endowing
{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{R}
with the Nygaard filtration, we obtain the corresponding Rees stack

R^{{\mathcal{N}}}:=\Re({{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{R}).

By
[
BS22
, 12.2]
, we have
{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{R}/\Fil^{1}_{{\mathcal{N}}}\cong R
, i.e.
(R^{{\mathcal{N}}})^{0}=B\mathbb{G}_{m,R}
. We write

R^{\Syn}:=({{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{R},I)^{\Syn}.

By
[
Bha23
, 5.5.11]
, the
R^{{\mathcal{N}}}
and the
R^{\Syn}
defined here are the algebraic versions of the stack
R^{{\mathcal{N}}}
and
R^{\Syn}
defined in loc. cit., where the formal spectrum (instead of the algebraic spectrum) of the Rees algebra is used in the definition.

Example 8.8
.

A special case of Example
8.7
is the Rees stack of an integral perfectoid ring
R
. In this case
({{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{R},(d))=(A_{\inf}(R),(d))
and
\varphi
is an isomorphism. Here
A_{\inf}(R):=W(R^{\flat})
is the ring of Witt vectors of the tilt
R^{\flat}
of
R
. Hence the Nygaard filtration is simply the
\varphi^{-1}(d)
-adic filtration on
A_{\inf}(R)
. Hence we are in the situation of Chapter
5
with
v
the inclusion
(\varphi^{-1}(d))\to W(R^{\flat})
. In particular
R^{{\mathcal{N}}}=\Re(W(R^{\flat})[t,u]/(tu-\varphi^{-1}(d)))
and Corollary
5.19
holds (note that
W(R^{\flat})
is a Dedekind domain if and only if
R
is a field).

Definition 8.9
.

Let
O
be a Dedekind domain, let
G
be a smooth affine group scheme over
O
, and let
\underline{A}
be a prism over
O
. A
prismatic
G
-gauge over
\underline{A}
is a
G
-bundle on
\underline{A}^{\Syn}
. If
R
is a qrsp ring we call a
G
-bundle over
R^{\Syn}
a
prismatic
G
-gauge over
R
.

Remark 8.10
.

Let
\underline{A}=(A,I,\delta)
be a prism. There is a natural map of frames from the Nygaard frame to the prismatic frame of
\underline{A}
which is defined as follows.

We claim that
\phi\colon A\to A
induces such a map. By definition,
\phi
sends
\Fil^{i}_{{\mathcal{N}}}
to
I^{i}
for all
i
. Hence we obtain a map of Rees stacks
\Re(A,I)\to\underline{A}^{{\mathcal{N}}}
. It remains to check that
\phi
is compatible with
\sigma
. For this we can work locally and assume that
I=(d)
. Then the commutativity of the following diagram shows the desired compatability since the horizontal maps induce the respective maps
\sigma

\textstyle{\Fil^{i}_{{\mathcal{N}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{I^{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a\mapsto a/d^{i}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{I^{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a\mapsto a/d^{i}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{A}

for
i\geq 0
.

This yields a map

(8.2.1)

\underline{A}^{\Disp}\longrightarrow\underline{A}^{\Syn}

which is functorial in
\underline{A}
.

By composition with the open immersion
\underline{A}^{\BK}\to\underline{A}^{\Disp}
we obtain a map
\underline{A}^{\BK}\to\underline{A}^{\Syn}
. Pullback via this map yields a functor from the groupoid of prismatic
G
-gauges over
\underline{A}
to the groupoid of Breuil–Kisin
G
-bundles. If
\underline{A}
is the prism attached to a quasi-regular semiperfectoid ring, then this is the functor constructed in
[
Ito24
, 8.2.11]
and applying the general criterion
[
Wed24
, 1.7]
to the Rees stacks and then using Corollary
4.9
one sees that this functor is fully faithful for flat affine group schemes
G
, recovering (and slightly generalizing) Ito’s result.

Appendix A
Colimits of Adams stacks

In this appendix we recall the complete and cocomplete 2-category of Adams stacks introduced by Schäppi. We prove that a Ferrand pushout of Adams schemes is a pushout in the category of Adams stacks. Recall the definition of an Adams stack.

Definition A.1
.

Let
R
be a ring. A qcqs fpqc-stack
{\mathcal{X}}
on
\mathcal{A}\textup{ff/$R$}
is called
Adams stack
if it satisfies the following conditions.

(a)

{\mathcal{X}}
has an affine diagonal.

(b)

{\mathcal{X}}
admits a faithfully flat morphism from an affine scheme

(c)

{\mathcal{X}}
has the resolution property (i.e., every quasi-coherent
{\mathscr{O}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
-module is the quotient of a filtered colimit of vector bundles).

An
R
-scheme is called
Adams scheme
if it is an Adams stack if considered as fpqc-stack.

Remark A.2
.

Let
R
be a ring. Let
{\mathcal{X}}
be an fpqc-stack on
\mathcal{A}\textup{ff/$R$}
that satisfies Conditions
(a)
and
(b)
from Definition
A.1
. Choose a faithfully flat morphism
X_{0}=\Spec A\to{\mathcal{X}}
and set
X_{1}=X_{0}\times_{{\mathcal{X}}}X_{0}
. Then
X_{1}
is an affine scheme by hypothesis and
(X_{0},X_{1})
has a canonical structure of a groupoid with flat source and target maps
X_{1}\to X_{0}
, given by the projections. Conversely, every groupoid consisting of affine schemes with flat source and target maps defines an fpqc-stack satisfying Conditions
(a)
and
(b)

[
Nau07
, Section 3.3]
.

Example A.3
.

Every quasi-compact scheme that has an ample family of line bundles is an Adams scheme as such schemes are automatically quasi-separated
[
Sta25
, 0FXS]
and have the resolution property
[
Sta25
, 0GMM]
.

The following two results give plenty of examples of Adams stacks.

Proposition A.4
.

Let
S
be an affine scheme and let
X
be a quasi-affine scheme over
S
. Let
G
be a geometrically reductive group scheme over
S
that admits a closed embedding into
\GL_{m}
for some
m
. Then the quotient stack
[G\backslash X]
is an Adams stack over
S
.

Proof.

Since
X
is quasi-affine, it has an affine diagonal. As
G\to S
is affine,
[G\backslash X]
has an affine diagonal and is a stack for the fpqc topology by
[
Wed24
, Section A.3]
. It has the resolution property by
[
Wed24
, Example A.29]
.
∎

Example A.5
.

(
[
Wed24
, Example A.28]
)
Let
R
be a regular ring of dimension
\leq 1
and let
G
be an affine flat group scheme of finite type over
R
. Then
BG
is an Adams stack over
R
.

We learned the following lemma from Eike Lau.

Lemma A.6
.

Let
f\colon{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{Y}}
be a relatively affine morphisms of prestacks and suppose that
{\mathcal{Y}}
is an Adams stack. Then
{\mathcal{X}}
is an Adams stack.

Proof.

Choose a faithfully flat morphism
Y_{0}=\Spec A\to{\mathcal{Y}}
and let
Y_{1}=Y_{0}\times_{{\mathcal{Y}}}Y_{0}
. Since
f
is affine,
X_{0}:=Y_{0}\times_{{\mathcal{Y}}}{\mathcal{X}}
and

X_{1}:=Y_{1}\times_{{\mathcal{Y}}}{\mathcal{X}}=X_{0}\times_{{\mathcal{X}}}X_{0}

are affine schemes and
(X_{0},X_{1})
has the structure of a groupoid with flat source and target map such that
{\mathcal{X}}
is the associated fpqc-stack (Remark
A.2
). Therefore
{\mathcal{X}}
satisfies Conditions
(a)
and
(b)
from Definition
A.1
.

It remains to check that
{\mathcal{X}}
has the resolution property. Let
{\mathscr{F}}
be a quasi-coherent module on
{\mathcal{X}}
. By our hypothesis on
{\mathcal{Y}}
, there exists a surjective homomorphism
\bigoplus{\mathscr{E}}_{i}\to f_{*}{\mathscr{F}}
, where
({\mathscr{E}}_{i})_{i}
is a family of vector bundles on
{\mathcal{Y}}
. We obtain a surjective homomorphism
\bigoplus f^{*}{\mathscr{E}}_{i}\to f^{*}f_{*}{\mathscr{F}}\to{\mathscr{F}}
, where the second map is surjective since
f
is affine.
∎

We have the following theorem by Schäppi.

Theorem A.7
.

(
[
Sch20
, 1.1.1]
and
[
Sch15
, 3.2.1, 3.2.6, 3.1.7]
)
Let
R
be a ring. The 2-category of Adams stacks over
R
is complete and cocomplete. Limits are formed as limits of prestacks.

Colimits can be recognized as follows. Let
{\mathcal{I}}
be a small connected category, let
{\mathcal{X}}\colon i\mapsto{\mathcal{X}}_{i}
be a diagram of non-empty Adams stacks indexed by
{\mathcal{I}}
, and let
(f_{i}\colon{\mathcal{X}}_{i}\to{\mathcal{W}})
be a pseudococone
4
4

4

We suppress 2-morphisms in the notation.
in the 2-category of Adams stacks on the diagram
{\mathcal{X}}
. Suppose that the following conditions hold.

(a)

The pseudocone obtained by applying
\textup{Vec}(-)
to the pseudococone
(f_{i})
induces an equivalence of
R
-linear categories

\textup{Vec}({\mathcal{W}})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\lim_{i}\textup{Vec}({\mathcal{X}}_{i}).

(b)

There exists a subset
I_{0}
of
\Ob({\mathcal{I}})
such that for any
{\mathcal{I}}
-diagram
i\mapsto{\mathcal{C}}_{i}
of categories the functor
\lim{\mathcal{C}}_{i}\to\prod_{i\in I_{0}}{\mathcal{C}}_{i}
reflects isomorphisms and such that for any module
{\mathscr{E}}
of finite presentation over
{\mathcal{W}}
with
f_{i}^{*}{\mathscr{E}}=0
for all
i\in I_{0}
one has
{\mathscr{E}}=0
.

Then the pseudocone induces an equivalence
\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{i}{\mathcal{X}}_{i}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{W}}
. Moreover, Condition
(a)
is also necessary.

Corollary A.8
.

Let

\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{Z^{\prime}}

be a diagram of Adams schemes. Suppose that
i
is a closed immersion and that
g
is an integral morphism such that every fiber of
g
is contained in an open affine subscheme of
Z
. Consider the Ferrand pushout of this diagram
[
Fer03
, 7.1]

\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{Z^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{\prime}}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}.}

Suppose that
X^{\prime}
is an Adams scheme. Then this is a pushout diagram in the 2-category of Adams stacks.

It is not clear to us whether the other hypotheses already imply that
X^{\prime}
is an Adams scheme.

Proof.

We use Theorem
A.7
. Condition
(a)
is satisfied by
[
TT16
, 6.3.3]
and Condition
(a)
holds by a globalization of
[
Fer03
, 2.2, ii)]
, see
[
Fer03
, 7.4]
.
∎

Appendix B
A flatness criterion

We will also use the following flatness criterion for which we could not find a reference. It is of course well known for noetherian rings.

Proposition B.1
.

Let
A
be a ring,
I\subseteq A
an ideal, let
M
be an
A
-module of finite presentation, and let
{\mathscr{E}}
be the corresponding quasi-coherent module on
X:=\Spec A
. Set
U:=X\setminus V(I)
. Then
{\mathscr{E}}
is a vector bundle if and only if the following conditions are satisfied.

(a)

M/IM
is a flat
A/I
-module and
\Tor^{A}_{1}(A/I,M)=0
.

(b)

{\mathscr{E}}{}_{|}{}_{U}
is a flat
{\mathscr{O}}_{U}
-module.

Proof.

Recall that
{\mathscr{E}}
is a vector bundle if and only if
{\mathscr{E}}
is flat and of finite presentation. Hence the conditions are necessary by standard flatness criterion (e.g.,
[
Bou98
, III,5.2]
). Now suppose that the two conditions hold. We have to show that for every maximal ideal
{\mathfrak{m}}\in V(I)
the
A_{{\mathfrak{m}}}
-module
M_{{\mathfrak{m}}}
is flat. As Tor commutes with flat base change we can replace
A
by
A_{{\mathfrak{m}}}
,
I
by
I_{{\mathfrak{m}}}
, and
M
by
M_{{\mathfrak{m}}}
. Therefore we may assume that
A
is local and that
I\neq A
. In this case the result follows from
[
Sta25
, 0471]
.
∎

Remark B.2
.

The proof shows that one can replace Condition
(b)
by the condition that
{\mathscr{E}}_{x}
is a flat
{\mathscr{O}}_{X,x}
-module for every closed point
x
of
\Spec A
contained in
U
. This conditions is for instance empty if
I
is contained in the Jacobson radical of
A
.

Remark B.3
.

The result
[
Sta25
, 0471]
used in the proof is much more general than the version needed to prove Proposition
B.1
. In fact a similar proof shows the following more general flatness criterion, which we will not need in the paper.

Let
A
be a ring,
I\subseteq A
an ideal, and let
A\to B
be an
A
-algebra of finite presentation. Set
X:=\Spec A
,
Y:=\Spec B
, and let
f\colon Y\to X
be the corresponding map of affine schemes. Let
M
be a
B
-module of finite presentation, and let
{\mathscr{E}}
be the corresponding quasi-coherent module on
Y
. Set
U:=X\setminus V(I)
. Suppose that the following conditions are satisfied.

(a)

M/IM
is a flat
A/I
-module and
\Tor^{A}_{1}(A/I,M)=0
.

(b)

{\mathscr{E}}{}_{|}{}_{f^{-1}(U)}
is a flat
{\mathscr{O}}_{U}
-module.

Then
{\mathscr{E}}
is flat over
X
.

Appendix C
Vector bundles on the display stack and
\GL_{h}
-displays

by Christopher Lang

In this appendix,
R
denotes a
p
-complete ring. Let
0\leq d\leq h
be integers and consider the standard minuscule cocharacter
\mu
of
\GL_{h}
given by

(C.0.1)

\mu\colon t\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}tI_{d}&\\
&I_{h-d}\end{pmatrix}

Our goal is to show that the category of vector bundles of rank
h
of type
\mu
on
R^{\Disp}
in the sense of
7.7
is equivalent to the category of
(\GL_{h},\mu)
-displays defined by Bültel and Pappas in
[
BP20
]
, see Proposition
C.4
. The latter category is very concisely described by Hoff in
[
Hof25
]
. Let us recall the definitions first. For any stack
{\mathcal{X}}
we denote by
\textup{Vec}^{h}({\mathcal{X}})
the category of vector bundles of rank
h
.

C.1.
Pairs and Displays of type
(h,d)

Definition C.1

(
[
Hof25
, 2.2]
)
.

A
pair
of type
(h,d)
over
R
is a tuple
(M,M_{1})
, where
M
is a finite projective
W(R)
-module of rank
h
, and
M_{1}\subseteq M
is a
W(R)
-submodule with
I_{R}M\subseteq M_{1}
and such that
M_{1}/I_{R}M\subseteq M/I_{R}M
is a rank
d
direct summand.

We can make this into a category
\mathrm{Pair}^{h,d}(R)
by setting morphisms
(M,M_{1})\to(M^{\prime},M^{\prime}_{1})
to be morphisms
f:M\to M^{\prime}
with
f(M_{1})\subseteq M^{\prime}_{1}
. (Equivalently:
\overline{f}(M_{1}/I_{R}M)\subseteq M^{\prime}_{1}/I_{R}M^{\prime}
)

For such a pair one has the following construction.

Proposition C.2

(
[
Hof25
, 2.7]
)
.

There is a functor

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pair}^{h,d}(R)

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Vec}^{h}\big(W(R)\big)

\displaystyle(M,M_{1})\cong(L\oplus T,L\oplus I_{R}T)

\displaystyle\mapsto\widetilde{M}_{1}\colon=L^{\phi}\oplus T^{\phi}

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}a^{\phi}&pb^{\phi}\\
\dot{c}&d^{\phi}\end{pmatrix}

with
\dot{c}:L^{\phi}\xrightarrow{c^{\phi}}I_{R}^{\phi}\underset{W(R)}{\otimes}T^{\prime\phi}\xrightarrow{\phi^{\mathrm{div}}\otimes\id}T^{\prime\phi}
. Here
M=L\oplus T
is a normal decomposition of the pair
(M,M_{1})
, see
[
Hof25
, 2.6]
.

Definition C.3

(
[
Hof25
, 2.9]
)
.

A
display

(M,M_{1},\Psi)
over
R
of type
(h,d)
consists of a pair
(M,M_{1})
of type
(h,d)
and an isomorphism
\Psi:\widetilde{M}_{1}\to M
. A morphism of displays is a morphism of pairs such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{M}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{M}_{1}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi^{\prime}}

commutes. We call the resulting category
\mathrm{Disp}^{h,d}(R)
.

The goal of this appendix is now to show that these constructions can be also described via the coequalizer diagram of stacks

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 26.50233pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-26.50233pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Spec W(R)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 31.25826pt\raise 6.65971pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\tau}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 50.50233pt\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 30.78522pt\raise-6.65971pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\sigma}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 50.50233pt\raise-2.15277pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 50.50233pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Re(W(R))}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\longrightarrow R^{\Disp}

constructed in Section
7
.

C.2.
Comparison of different notions of displays

Denote by
\textup{Vec}^{h}\big(\Re(W(R))\big)^{\mu}
the category of vector bundles of rank
h
and of type
\mu
over
\Re(W(R))
with
\mu
as defined in (
C.0.1
). Similarly define
\textup{Vec}^{h}\big(R^{\Disp})\big)^{\mu}
.

Proposition C.4
.

There are equivalences of categories which are functorial in
R
.

\displaystyle\textup{Vec}^{h}\big(\Re(W(R))\big)^{\mu}

\displaystyle\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathrm{Pair}^{h,d}(R),

\displaystyle\textup{Vec}^{h}\big(R^{\Disp})\big)^{\mu}

\displaystyle\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathrm{Disp}^{h,d}(R).

Via these equivalences

(1)

pullback of vector bundles via
\Re(W(R))\to R^{\Disp}
is identified with the functor sending a display to its underlying pair,

(2)

pullback via
\tau
is identified with sending a pair
(M,M_{1})
to its underlying
W(R)
-module
M
,

(3)

pullback via
\sigma
is identified with sending a pair
(M,M_{1})
to the
W(R)
-module
\tilde{M}_{1}
.

To prove the proposition, we first spell out what it means for a vector bundle of
\Re(W(R))
to be of type
\mu
.

Remark C.5
.

By
[
Wed24
, 2.6]
, we have

H^{1}(\Re(W(R)),\GL_{h})\xrightarrow{\sim}H^{1}(B\mathbb{G}_{m,R},\GL_{h}),

and objects on the right are (classes of) finite projective graded modules on
R
(where
R
has the trivial grading), and these are isomorphic to
\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\overline{L}_{i}\otimes_{R}R(i)
, where the
\overline{L}_{i}
are vector bundles on
R
, with ranks adding to
h
. Now we only take such objects with
\rank\overline{L}_{1}=h-d
and
\rank\overline{L}_{0}=d
. The vector bundle on
\Re(W(R))
will then be isomorphic to
\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}L_{i}\otimes_{W(R)}\Rees\bigl(W(R)\bigr)(i)
, where
L_{i}
is a lift of
\overline{L}_{i}
to a
W(R)
-module, unique up to isomorphism, using that
(W(R),I_{R})
is Henselian.

Let us elaborate on the notation
L\otimes_{W(R)}\Rees\bigl(W(R)\bigr)(i)
, as we will need it often in the sequel. We first make
L
into a
\Rees\bigl(W(R)\bigr)
-module and then shift by
i
. In particular, we have

\left(L\underset{W(R)}{\otimes}\Rees\bigl(W(R)\bigr)(i)\right)_{j}=\begin{cases}L,&j\leq-i\\
I_{R}L,&j>-i,\end{cases}

where multiplication with
t
is given by the identity for
j\leq-i
, the inclusion
I_{R}L\hookrightarrow L
for
j=-i+1
and multiplication with
p
otherwise. Multiplication with
V(x)t^{-k}
is given by
V(x)t^{-k}\cdot l=V(x)l
for
j\leq-i
and by
V(x)t^{-k}\cdot V(y)l=V(xy)l
otherwise.

Next we define a functor

(C.2.1)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Vec}^{h}\big(\Re(W(R))\big)^{\mu}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Pair}^{h,d}(R),

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle\mapsto(N_{-1},N_{0})

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\mapsto f_{-1}=f|_{N_{-1}}

Let us first check that this is well defined: We first show that
N_{-1}
is a projective
W(R)
-module of rank
h
. Note that it is a
W(R)
-module as
W(R)
is the degree 0 part of
\Rees\bigl(W(R)\bigr)
. It is finite projective because we can identify

N\cong\left(L_{0}\underset{W(R)}{\otimes}\Rees\bigl(W(R)\bigr)\right)\oplus\left(L_{1}\underset{W(R)}{\otimes}\Rees\bigl(W(R)\bigr)(1)\right),

where
L_{0}
and
L_{1}
are projective
W(R)
-modules of rank
d
resp
h-d
. Then we get

N_{-1}\cong L_{0}\oplus L_{1}

as
W(R)
-modules, and this is projective of rank
h
.

Next we need to identify
N_{0}
as a submodule of
N_{-1}
. This is done by the multiplication with
t
map. This map is injective, since with the identification above we have

N_{0}\cong L_{0}\oplus I_{R}L_{1},

as
I_{R}L_{1}=L_{1}\underset{W(R)}{\otimes}\Rees\bigl(W(R)\bigr)_{1}
, so
L_{0}
and
L_{1}
form a normal decomposition of
(N_{-1},N_{0})
.

The condition
I_{R}N_{-1}\subseteq N_{0}
follows because
I_{R}
naturally lives in degree 1 inside
\Rees\bigl(W(R)\bigr)
. Alternatively, this also immediately follows from the identifications above. We still need that
N_{0}/I_{R}N_{-1}\subseteq N_{-1}/I_{R}N_{-1}
is a direct summand of rank
d
. But this we get from the identification, as

N_{0}/I_{R}N_{-1}\cong L_{0}/I_{R}L_{0}\qquad\text{and}\qquad N_{-1}/I_{R}N_{-1}\cong L_{0}/I_{R}L_{0}\oplus L_{1}/I_{R}L_{1}.

Well-definedness on morphisms is clear, using that
f_{-1}(t\cdot N_{0})=t\cdot f_{0}(N_{0})\subseteq t\cdot N_{0}^{\prime}
.

It is easy to see that the functor (
C.2.1
) is functorial in
R
. Next we show:

Lemma C.6
.

The functor (
C.2.1
) is an equivalence.

Proof.

Let us first show that the functor is essentially surjective. Given a pair
(M,M_{1})
, we can take a normal decomposition
M=L\oplus T
and
M_{1}=L\oplus I_{R}T
. Then

N\colon=\left(L\underset{W(R)}{\otimes}\Rees\bigl(W(R)\bigr)\right)\oplus\left(T\underset{W(R)}{\otimes}\Rees\bigl(W(R)\bigr)(1)\right)

is a preimage of this pair.

Next we show that the functor is full. Let’s take two graded modules
N,N^{\prime}
of type
\mu
, with decompositions

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle\cong\left(L\underset{W(R)}{\otimes}\Rees\bigl(W(R)\bigr)\right)

\displaystyle\oplus\left(T\underset{W(R)}{\otimes}\Rees\bigl(W(R)\bigr)(1)\right),

\displaystyle N^{\prime}

\displaystyle\cong\left(L^{\prime}\underset{W(R)}{\otimes}\Rees\bigl(W(R)\bigr)\right)

\displaystyle\oplus\left(T^{\prime}\underset{W(R)}{\otimes}\Rees\bigl(W(R)\bigr)(1)\right).

A map of pairs corresponds to a quadruple

a:L\to L^{\prime},\quad b:T\to L^{\prime},\quad c:L\to I_{R}T^{\prime},\quad d:T\to T^{\prime},

and this data can be made into a map
N\to N^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle a\otimes\id

\displaystyle:L\underset{W(R)}{\otimes}\Rees\bigl(W(R)\bigr)

\displaystyle\to L^{\prime}\underset{W(R)}{\otimes}\Rees\bigl(W(R)\bigr)

\displaystyle d\otimes\id

\displaystyle:T\underset{W(R)}{\otimes}\Rees\bigl(W(R)\bigr)(1)

\displaystyle\to T^{\prime}\underset{W(R)}{\otimes}\Rees\bigl(W(R)\bigr)(1)

\displaystyle b\otimes\cdot t

\displaystyle:T\underset{W(R)}{\otimes}\Rees\bigl(W(R)\bigr)(1)

\displaystyle\to L^{\prime}\underset{W(R)}{\otimes}\Rees\bigl(W(R)\bigr),

and for the last map we note that each graded piece of
T^{\prime}\underset{W(R)}{\otimes}\Rees\bigl(W(R)\bigr)(1)
is either
T^{\prime}
(in degrees
\leq-1
), or
I_{R}T^{\prime}
. Therefore we can define a map

\displaystyle\tilde{c}

\displaystyle:L\underset{W(R)}{\otimes}\Rees\bigl(W(R)\bigr)

\displaystyle\to T^{\prime}\underset{W(R)}{\otimes}\Rees\bigl(W(R)\bigr)(1),

which is induced by
c
in degrees
\leq 0
, and in degrees
\geq 1
by

I_{R}L\to I_{R}T^{\prime},\qquad V(x)\cdot l\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n_{l}}V(xy_{i})\cdot a_{i},\qquad(\text{with }c(l)=\sum_{i=1}^{n_{l}}V(y_{i})\cdot a_{i}\in I_{R}T^{\prime}).

We need to check that this is well-defined, namely that two different representations of
c(l)
yield the same result. By subtraction, it is enough to show that if
c(l)=0
, then
V(x)\cdot l
maps to
0
. If
p
is not a zero-divisor in
W(R)
, we can multiply by
p
, use
pV(xy_{i})=V(x)V(y_{i})
and pull
V(x)
outside the sum. Otherwise, the situation seems to be more complicated. We would like to use
V(y_{i})a_{i}=V(y_{i}F(a_{i}))
, but we only have the Frobenius in
W(R)
, not on
T^{\prime}
, so we first need to embed
T^{\prime}
into a free module: We can look at

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{c}:\quad L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{R}T^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{R}^{m}=I_{R}T^{\prime}\oplus I_{R}\tilde{T}^{\prime},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

where
T^{\prime}\oplus\tilde{T}^{\prime}
is free. We can write the
j
-th component of this map as
l\mapsto V(c_{j}(l))
. We can make this into a map

\tilde{c}^{\prime}:I_{R}L\to I_{R}^{m},\quad V(x)\cdot l\mapsto\biggl(V\bigl(xc_{j}(l)\bigr)\biggr)_{1\leq j\leq m},

which is clearly well-defined. Once we show that
\tilde{c}^{\prime}=\iota\circ\tilde{c}
, we know that
\tilde{c}
is also well-defined. This equation holds because we have

\hat{c}(l)=\sum_{i}V(y_{i})a_{i}=\left(\sum_{i}V(y_{i})a_{ij}\right)_{j}=\biggl(V\bigl(\underbrace{\sum_{i}y_{i}F(a_{ij})}_{=c_{j}(l)}\bigr)\biggr)_{j},

where
a_{ij}
are the components of
a_{i}
in
I_{R}^{m}
, so we can compute

\displaystyle\tilde{c}^{\prime}(V(x)l)_{j}

\displaystyle=V(xc_{j}(l))=V\left(\sum_{i}xy_{i}F(a_{ij})\right)

\displaystyle\iota\circ\tilde{c}(V(x)l)_{j}

\displaystyle=\iota\left(\sum_{i}V(xy_{i})\cdot a_{i}\right)_{j}=\sum_{i}V(xy_{i})\cdot a_{ij}=V\left(\sum_{i}xy_{i}F(a_{ij})\right).

This shows that the functor is full.

It remains to see that the functor is faithful. We need to show that the restriction map

g:\Hom_{\Rees(W(R))}(N,N^{\prime})\to\Hom_{W(R)}(N_{-1},N^{\prime}_{-1})

is injective.
For this we take
W(R)
-modules
\tilde{L},\tilde{L}^{\prime},\tilde{T},\tilde{T}^{\prime}
such that
L\oplus\tilde{L}
etc are all free (of rank
n_{L},n_{L^{\prime}},n_{T},n_{T^{\prime}}
). Then we look at the map

g\oplus\tilde{g}:\Hom_{\Rees(W(R))}(N\oplus\tilde{N},N^{\prime}\oplus\tilde{N}^{\prime})\to\Hom_{W(R)}((N\oplus\tilde{N})_{-1},(N^{\prime}\oplus\tilde{N}^{\prime})_{-1}),

where
\tilde{N}
resp.
\tilde{N}^{\prime}
are defined with
\tilde{L},\tilde{T}
resp.
\tilde{L}^{\prime},\tilde{T}^{\prime}
, and observe that

N\oplus\tilde{N}=\Rees\bigl(W(R)\bigr)^{n_{L}}\oplus\Rees\bigl(W(R)\bigr)^{n_{T}}(1).

Using
\Hom_{R}(A^{n},B^{m})=\Hom_{R}(A,B)^{n\times m}
, we can eliminate the products, as
g\oplus\tilde{g}
maps each matrix entry separately. So we need to check that maps of the form

\displaystyle\Hom_{\Rees(W(R))}\Big(\Rees\bigl(W(R)\bigr)(\epsilon),\Rees\bigl(W(R)\bigr)(\delta)\Big)

\displaystyle\to\Hom_{W(R)}\big(W(R),W(R)\big)=W(R),

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\mapsto f|_{-1}

are injective for
\epsilon,\delta\in\{0,1\}
. But maps on the left are determined by the image
f(1)
of the element
1\in\Rees\bigl(W(R)\bigr)
, and
f(1)
has to be a homogeneous of degree
\epsilon-\delta
, i.e.
-1
,
0
or
1
. Hence the map is in fact the natural map from the degree
-1
,
0
or
1
of
\Rees\bigl(W(R)\bigr)
to
W(R)
, which is indeed injective. Hence
g\oplus\tilde{g}
is injective, and therefore
g
as well.
∎

To finish the proof of Proposition
C.4
it now suffices to show the following lemma.

Lemma C.7
.

Via the equivalence (
C.2.1
) the pullback via
\tau
is identified with the functor
(M,M_{1})\mapsto M
and the pullback via
\sigma
is identified with
(M,M_{1})\mapsto\tilde{M}_{1}
.

Let us recall the definition of
\tau,\sigma\colon\Spec W(R)\to\Re(W(R))
from Section
7.1
. By the description of quotient stacks, both maps correspond to line bundles
L
on
\Spec W(R)
together with equivariant maps
L\to\Spec\Rees\bigl(W(R)\bigr)
, or in the language of modules, a rank 1 projective module
A
over
W(R)
and a graded map
\Rees\bigl(W(R)\bigr)\to\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}A^{\otimes i}\cdot t^{-i}
. For our two special points we choose
A=W(R)
both times, and for the graded maps we have

†
†
nicematrix-placeholder:
NiceArray (nicematrix)

In Section
7.1
,
\tilde{\sigma}
is denoted by
\sigma^{*}
.

Proof.

If
N=\left(L\underset{W(R)}{\otimes}\Rees\bigl(W(R)\bigr)\right)\oplus\left(T\underset{W(R)}{\otimes}\Rees\bigl(W(R)\bigr)(1)\right)
, then we have to show

\displaystyle\tau^{*}N

\displaystyle\quad\text{corresponds to}\quad L\oplus T\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\sigma^{*}N

\displaystyle\quad\text{corresponds to}\quad L^{\phi}\oplus T^{\phi}.

To see this, we look at the diagram

(
[\Spec W(R)[t^{\pm 1}]/\mathbb{G}_{m}]
-QCoh)
(
\Spec W(R)
-QCoh)
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{($\Re(W(R))$-QCoh)}.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\mapsto\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}M\cdot t^{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N\otimes\tilde{\tau}\mapsfrom N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau^{*}N\mapsfrom N}

So in order to compute
\tau^{*}N
, we only need to compute any degree of
N\otimes\tilde{\tau}^{*}
. But given the decomposition into
L
and
T
, this is an easy task, as

\left(L\underset{W(R)}{\otimes}\Rees\bigl(W(R)\bigr)\right)\underset{\Rees(W(R)),\tilde{\tau}}{\otimes}W(R)[t^{\pm 1}]=L\underset{W(R)}{\otimes}W(R)[t^{\pm 1}]=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}L\cdot t^{-i}.

(This uses that
W(R)\to\Rees\bigl(W(R)\bigr)\xrightarrow{\tilde{\tau}}W(R)[t^{\pm 1}]
is the canonical map
x\mapsto xt^{0}
.) For the
T
-component basically the same calculation holds true, just the degrees are shifted by 1. This shows
\tau^{*}N=L\oplus T
. It remains to show
\sigma^{*}N=L^{\phi}\oplus T^{\phi}
.

For
\sigma^{*}N
we do the same computation, but with
\tau
replaced by
\sigma
everywhere.

\left(L\underset{W(R)}{\otimes}\Rees\bigl(W(R)\bigr)\right)\underset{\Rees(W(R)),\tilde{\sigma}}{\otimes}W(R)[t^{\pm 1}]=L\underset{W(R),\phi}{\otimes}W(R)[t^{\pm 1}]=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}L^{\phi}\cdot t^{-i}

with
\phi:W(R)\to\Rees\bigl(W(R)\bigr)\xrightarrow{\tilde{\sigma}}W(R)[t^{\pm 1}],\;\;x\mapsto\phi(x)t^{0}
. Again, up to shift, the same holds for
T
. This shows the claim, and hence the map
\Psi
from displays and
\psi
from Rees stacks can be identified with each other through the choice of a normal decomposition.

We still need to check compatibility on morphisms, i.e. that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\phi}\oplus T^{\phi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L\oplus T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\prime\phi}\oplus T^{\prime\phi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\prime}\oplus T^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a^{\phi}&pb^{\phi}\\
\dot{c}&d^{\phi}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi^{\prime}}

commutes if and only if

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{*}N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau^{*}N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{*}N^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau^{*}N^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{*}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\otimes\id&b\otimes\cdot t\\
\tilde{c}&d\otimes\id\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau^{*}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\otimes\id&b\otimes\cdot t\\
\tilde{c}&d\otimes\id\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime}}

commutes. For this we check that both

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L\oplus T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau^{*}N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\prime}\oplus T^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau^{*}N^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau^{*}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\otimes\id&b\otimes\cdot t\\
\tilde{c}&d\otimes\id\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\phi}\oplus T^{\phi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{*}N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\prime\phi}\oplus T^{\prime\phi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{*}N^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a^{\phi}&pb^{\phi}\\
\dot{c}&d^{\phi}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{*}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\otimes\id&b\otimes\cdot t\\
\tilde{c}&d\otimes\id\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

commute. The essential step is checking the ”
c
”-component on the right. This we do by looking at the following diagram, where we use that
c(l)
can be written as (a sum of)
V(y_{l})\cdot a_{l}
, for
l\in L
. The index 0 or 1 stands for the homogeneous part of the corresponding degree of the graded module. For
T^{\prime}
, we have degree 1 due to the shift.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l\otimes x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l\otimes x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{L^{\phi}=L\underset{W(R),\phi}{\otimes}W(R)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left(L\underset{W(R),\phi}{\otimes}W(R)[t^{\pm 1}]\right)_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V(y_{l})\otimes x)\otimes(a_{l}\otimes 1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big(I_{R}\otimes_{\phi}W(R)\big)\underset{W(R)}{\otimes}\big(T^{\prime}\otimes_{\phi}W(R)\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left(L\underset{W(R)}{\otimes}\Rees\bigl(W(R)\bigr)\otimes_{\tilde{\sigma}}W(R)[t^{\pm 1}]\right)_{0}}=\sigma^{*}L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l\otimes 1\otimes x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{l}\otimes y_{l}x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{\prime}\otimes_{\phi}W(R)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left(T^{\prime}\underset{W(R)}{\otimes}\Rees\bigl(W(R)\bigr)\otimes_{\tilde{\sigma}}W(R)[t^{\pm 1}]\right)_{1}}=\sigma^{*}T^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{l}\otimes V(y_{l})t\inv\otimes x}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left(T^{\prime}\underset{W(R),\phi}{\otimes}W(R)[t^{\pm 1}]\right)_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{l}\otimes y_{l}xt\inv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l\otimes x\mapsto l\otimes x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{subarray}{c}l\otimes x\\

(V(y_{l})\otimes x)\otimes(a_{l}\otimes 1)\end{subarray}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dot{c}}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{subarray}{c}l\otimes\sigma(x)y\\

l\otimes x\otimes y\end{subarray}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{subarray}{c}(V(y)\otimes x_{1})\otimes(m\otimes x_{2})\\

m\otimes yx_{1}x_{2}\end{subarray}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\mathrm{div}}\otimes\id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{*}\tilde{c}}

\tilde{c}(l\otimes x)\otimes y\end{subarray}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m\otimes x\mapsto m\otimes xt\inv}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{subarray}{c}m\otimes x\otimes y\\

∎
